What you will practice · Choose your pace

What you will practice

  • Reconcile apparently conflicting metrics by stating the event, distinct entity, window, and denominator.
  • Explain mixed source freshness and set an appropriate reporting boundary without mislabeling missing data as zero.
  • Turn an isolated monthly count into a clear performance statement with definition, target, comparison, and units.
  • Report a meaningful association while separating the observed difference from an unestablished causal effect.
  • Explain row grain and join multiplicity, diagnose a duplicated aggregate, and reject a misleading distinct-value fix.

Choose your pace

Quick practice · 15 minutes: complete one short exchange, check the explanations, and read both roles aloud.

Extended practice · two or three sittings: check the case facts, study the vocabulary and phrases, complete the longer conversation, then rehearse the follow-up exchange.

Level guide: designed for intermediate to advanced learners. B1 learners can start with the short exchanges and supplied phrases; B2 learners can complete the cloze conversations; C1 learners can focus on the distinctions in the language checks. These are teaching suggestions, not a certified level assessment.

01Metric Definitions and Business QuestionsTwo active-user totals, two different events+

Your goal

Reconcile apparently conflicting metrics by stating the event, distinct entity, window, and denominator.

This lesson in the book, page 4 ↗

A Read the situation

Two active-user totals, two different events

Analyst Mei and product lead Rafael compare September figures for the same 2,000 eligible users, using UTC calendar boundaries and excluding internal and test accounts. One report counts 1,200 distinct users with a successful login. Another counts 900 distinct users with a completed transaction. All 900 are within the login group. Both queries match their stated definitions. The business question is how many eligible users completed a transaction, not how many signed in. The team must choose an accurate label and rate.

Mei: Data analyst / Rafael: Product lead

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. Which count answers the supplied business question?

Answer and explanation

A. 900 distinct users with a completed transaction

The question concerns eligible users completing transactions, which the second measure directly counts.

2. What is the correct eligible-user transaction rate?

Answer and explanation

C. 45%

Nine hundred divided by two thousand eligible users equals forty-five percent.

3. Why can both query results be correct?

Answer and explanation

D. They apply different event definitions to the same eligible population.

Distinct event definitions can produce different accurate totals without a calculation error.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

900/2,000 is 45% of eligible users. 900/1,200 is 75% of users who logged in. Both calculations are valid for their stated bases, but only the first answers the supplied eligible-user question.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
metric
A defined quantitative measure.define a metric
business question
The decision-relevant question an analysis is meant to answer.clarify the business question
metric definition
Rules specifying exactly how a measure is calculated.agree a metric definition
qualifying event
An action meeting the condition for inclusion.specify the qualifying event
distinct user
A unique user counted once under a defined identity rule.count distinct users
event count
The number of recorded qualifying occurrences.distinguish event counts from users
eligible population
The complete group allowed into the measure's base.define the eligible population
denominator
The total used as the base of a rate.state the denominator
numerator
The counted outcome placed over the base of a rate.verify the numerator
reporting window
The time period included in a measure.align reporting windows
calendar month
A month bounded by the adopted calendar and time zone.report by calendar month
time zone
The time reference used to assign event dates.fix the reporting time zone
exclusion rule
A condition removing records from the measure.document exclusion rules
internal account
An account belonging to the organization's own personnel or systems.exclude internal accounts
test account
An account used for testing rather than genuine activity.filter test accounts
deduplication
Removing repeated representations of the same counted entity.apply user deduplication
identity resolution
Connecting records that refer to the same entity.review identity resolution
active user
A user meeting a specified activity definition.define active users explicitly
login-based activity
Activity defined by a qualifying sign-in.report login-based activity
transaction-based activity
Activity defined by a qualifying transaction.report transaction-based activity
subset
A group entirely contained within another group.identify the subset relationship
metric owner
The role accountable for a measure's definition and use.identify the metric owner
semantic layer
Shared definitions connecting data to business meaning.maintain a semantic layer
reconciliation
Explaining and resolving differences between reported figures.reconcile metric totals

The two groups overlap: all 900 transacting users are among the 1,200 login users. Adding them would double-count those 900. Repeated transactions by one person also do not increase a distinct-user total.

C Notice the language

Treat a disagreement as a definition check

When two teams defend different totals, ask what each number counts before deciding which team made an error. Preserve useful measures with accurate names. A measure can be correctly calculated yet poorly suited to the business question on the table.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Clarify the question
"Are we measuring sign-in or completed transactions?"
Define the counted entity
"Each user is counted once."
Name the event
"The qualifying event is a completed transaction."
Set the window
"Use the September calendar month in UTC."
Keep exclusions aligned
"Both measures exclude internal and test accounts."
Acknowledge both results
"Both queries match their own definitions."
Explain the difference
"The reports use different activity events."
Choose the relevant count
"Nine hundred users answer the transaction question."
State the base
"The eligible population is 2,000 users."
Give the rate
"That is 45% of eligible users."
Distinguish another rate
"Seventy-five percent uses logged-in users as its base."
Avoid double-counting
"The transaction group is a subset of the login group."
Improve the label
"Call this transaction-active users."
Preserve the other measure
"Keep the login measure under its own label."
Assign definition ownership
"The metric owner should approve the shared definition."
Close the disagreement
"We need consistent meaning, not an artificial match between different events."
6 language and precision notes
Active
Incomplete unless the qualifying event is stated.
Users
Distinct identities, not the number of actions.
Of eligible users
Fixes the denominator for the rate.
During September
Needs consistent calendar boundaries and time zone.
Correct
Can mean calculated correctly without being decision-relevant.
Reconcile
Explain differences before forcing totals to match.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which label best fits the 900 count?

Answer and explanation

A. September transaction-active users

The count concerns distinct users with a completed transaction in the reporting window.

2. What does 900/1,200 measure?

Answer and explanation

B. The share of logged-in users who completed a transaction

The denominator is the login group, and the numerator is its transacting subset.

3. Why should the two totals not be added?

Answer and explanation

B. The 900 transacting users are already included among the 1,200 login users.

The supplied subset relationship means adding totals double-counts the transacting group.

4. Which next step addresses the real disagreement?

Answer and explanation

D. Record both event definitions and select the transaction measure for this question.

The discrepancy is semantic and decision-related, not a demonstrated query failure.

E Conversations

Two active-user totals, two different events

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Mei: Data analyst / Rafael: Product lead

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • reconciliation
  • qualifying event
  • metric owner
  • business question
  • reporting window
  • subset
  • distinct user
  • denominator
  • numerator
  • semantic layer
  1. Rafael
    The executive slide says twelve hundred active users, but Finance has nine hundred. Both teams insist their query is right. Which number should I remove?
  2. Mei
    First agree the 1. We need the number of eligible users who completed a transaction during September, not merely the number who signed in.
  3. Rafael
    The twelve hundred report counts successful logins, once per user. Finance counts completed transactions, also once per user. Both exclude internal and test accounts.
  4. Mei
    Then the 2 differs. The queries can both be correct while answering different questions. We should not change a valid calculation simply to make two different meanings agree.
  5. Rafael
    The window is the September calendar month in UTC for both, and they use the same two thousand eligible users. That part is aligned.
  6. Mei
    Good. A shared 3 removes one possible source of disagreement. The distinction here is activity type, not different month boundaries or different treatment of test accounts.
  7. Rafael
    For the actual question, I should choose nine hundred and label it as people completing a transaction. Multiple transactions by one user still count once.
  8. Mei
    Yes, the entity is a 4. That is not the total number of transaction events. The slide should make the counted entity clear enough that the audience cannot silently substitute another.
  9. Rafael
    I have seventy-five percent on the slide: nine hundred divided by twelve hundred. Is that answering the eligible-user question, or have I changed the base?
  10. Mei
    That uses a different 5. Nine hundred divided by two thousand eligible users is forty-five percent. Nine hundred divided by twelve hundred logged-in users is seventy-five percent.
  11. Rafael
    Both rates tell us something, but forty-five percent is the answer to the eligible-user question. Seventy-five percent describes the logged-in group instead.
  12. Mei
    Exactly. Keep the 6 and base together in speech. Saying just the percentage would hide the change in meaning and invite an incorrect comparison later.
  13. Rafael
    Someone suggested adding the two reports to show total engagement. But the nine hundred transacting users are already included in the twelve hundred who logged in.
  14. Mei
    That 7 relationship makes addition inappropriate for distinct people. It would count the transaction group twice rather than identify twenty-one hundred different active users.
  15. Rafael
    I will keep the login figure because it is useful, but give it a different label. The executive headline will use the transaction definition.
  16. Mei
    Put both in the 8 with the event, identity rule, exclusions, and time zone. Renaming one slide is not enough if other reports keep reusing the ambiguous active-user label.
  17. Rafael
    Who signs off if the transaction event changes next month? I do not want the same label to hide a new definition.
  18. Mei
    Assign a 9 and record the approved version. That gives both teams a place to resolve changes before comparing totals that no longer mean the same thing.
  19. Rafael
    My summary will be nine hundred transaction-active users, forty-five percent of two thousand eligible users, September UTC. The login measure remains separately labeled.
  20. Mei
    That completes the 10. We have preserved both correct calculations, selected the measure that answers the decision, and removed the ambiguity that created the apparent conflict.

Answers and explanations
  1. business question Turn 2. The business question determines which correctly calculated measure is relevant to the decision.
  2. qualifying event Turn 4. The qualifying event differs between successful login and completed transaction, explaining the distinct totals.
  3. reporting window Turn 6. The reporting window fixes the period and prevents a time-boundary difference from being confused with the event-definition difference.
  4. distinct user Turn 8. A distinct user is counted once even when the user performs multiple qualifying actions within the window.
  5. denominator Turn 10. The denominator defines the rate's base; eligible users and logged-in users answer different percentage questions.
  6. numerator Turn 12. The numerator remains nine hundred transacting users while the stated denominator changes the meaning of the percentage.
  7. subset Turn 14. The subset relationship means every transacting user is already counted within the login group, so addition would duplicate those users.
  8. semantic layer Turn 16. The semantic layer preserves shared business definitions so later reports use the same labels and rules consistently.
  9. metric owner Turn 18. The metric owner is accountable for the definition and its controlled evolution rather than leaving each report to invent its own meaning.
  10. reconciliation Turn 20. Reconciliation explains the different totals and their uses without forcing distinct measures to produce an artificial match.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Rafael: The executive slide says twelve hundred active users, but Finance has nine hundred. Both teams insist their query is right. Which number should I remove?
  2. Mei: First agree the business question. We need the number of eligible users who completed a transaction during September, not merely the number who signed in.
  3. Rafael: The twelve hundred report counts successful logins, once per user. Finance counts completed transactions, also once per user. Both exclude internal and test accounts.
  4. Mei: Then the qualifying event differs. The queries can both be correct while answering different questions. We should not change a valid calculation simply to make two different meanings agree.
  5. Rafael: The window is the September calendar month in UTC for both, and they use the same two thousand eligible users. That part is aligned.
  6. Mei: Good. A shared reporting window removes one possible source of disagreement. The distinction here is activity type, not different month boundaries or different treatment of test accounts.
  7. Rafael: For the actual question, I should choose nine hundred and label it as people completing a transaction. Multiple transactions by one user still count once.
  8. Mei: Yes, the entity is a distinct user. That is not the total number of transaction events. The slide should make the counted entity clear enough that the audience cannot silently substitute another.
  9. Rafael: I have seventy-five percent on the slide: nine hundred divided by twelve hundred. Is that answering the eligible-user question, or have I changed the base?
  10. Mei: That uses a different denominator. Nine hundred divided by two thousand eligible users is forty-five percent. Nine hundred divided by twelve hundred logged-in users is seventy-five percent.
  11. Rafael: Both rates tell us something, but forty-five percent is the answer to the eligible-user question. Seventy-five percent describes the logged-in group instead.
  12. Mei: Exactly. Keep the numerator and base together in speech. Saying just the percentage would hide the change in meaning and invite an incorrect comparison later.
  13. Rafael: Someone suggested adding the two reports to show total engagement. But the nine hundred transacting users are already included in the twelve hundred who logged in.
  14. Mei: That subset relationship makes addition inappropriate for distinct people. It would count the transaction group twice rather than identify twenty-one hundred different active users.
  15. Rafael: I will keep the login figure because it is useful, but give it a different label. The executive headline will use the transaction definition.
  16. Mei: Put both in the semantic layer with the event, identity rule, exclusions, and time zone. Renaming one slide is not enough if other reports keep reusing the ambiguous active-user label.
  17. Rafael: Who signs off if the transaction event changes next month? I do not want the same label to hide a new definition.
  18. Mei: Assign a metric owner and record the approved version. That gives both teams a place to resolve changes before comparing totals that no longer mean the same thing.
  19. Rafael: My summary will be nine hundred transaction-active users, forty-five percent of two thousand eligible users, September UTC. The login measure remains separately labeled.
  20. Mei: That completes the reconciliation. We have preserved both correct calculations, selected the measure that answers the decision, and removed the ambiguity that created the apparent conflict.
Dialogue in the book, page 10 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Active by login or completed transaction?

Fictional metric discussion using the lesson case: two teams report different active-user totals because one counts logins and the other completed transactions.

  1. Operations manager: Your active-user total differs from the product team's. Which dashboard should we call the source of truth?
  2. BI analyst: First clarify the business definition. Their metric counts logins, while ours counts users completing transactions.
  3. Operations manager: Then the disagreement isn't necessarily a calculation error; the label hides two different activities.
  4. BI analyst: Exactly. Which business question do you need the number to answer in this meeting?
  5. Operations manager: I want to know how many people completed the service task, not merely entered the system.
  6. BI analyst: Then transaction-based activity is relevant, but we should still confirm its period and counting rules.
  7. Operations manager: Could both measures remain useful if their names distinguish them rather than competing for one vague label?
  8. BI analyst: Yes. Assign an owner to each definition and avoid presenting the totals as interchangeable.
  9. Operations manager: I'll ask the product team to review the labels with us before we publish another comparison.
  10. BI analyst: I'll bring the current definitions and mark any unresolved exclusions so the discussion stays precise.
An order placed is not an order fulfilled

Fictional operations reporting: sales counts orders placed, while fulfillment counts orders shipped; both reports use the label orders this week.

  1. Sales operations lead: Our weekly order count is higher than fulfillment's. Could the warehouse be missing records?
  2. Analytics lead: Possibly, but first check the event definition. Sales counts placement, and fulfillment counts shipment.
  3. Sales operations lead: So an order placed this week may belong to another week's shipment count.
  4. Analytics lead: Correct. We need the business question before deciding which metric is appropriate.
  5. Sales operations lead: For staffing, the warehouse wants the work actually shipped during the week, not demand arriving.
  6. Analytics lead: Then use the shipment-based definition for that purpose and retain placement counts for demand analysis.
  7. Sales operations lead: Could you also clarify whether a split shipment counts once or more than once?
  8. Analytics lead: That rule isn't established here. I'll ask the metric owner rather than assume a counting grain.
  9. Sales operations lead: Let's label the two reports clearly and keep the split-shipment question open until confirmed.
  10. Analytics lead: Agreed. A source of truth needs an explicit definition, not simply a preferred dashboard name.
Revenue before or after refunds?

Fictional reporting clarification: two internal summaries use revenue but one subtracts refunds; tax treatment and reporting period are unspecified.

  1. Commercial manager: These revenue totals disagree. Could you clarify whether both include the same transactions and adjustments?
  2. BI analyst: One subtracts refunds, while the other doesn't. Tax treatment and reporting period also need confirmation.
  3. Commercial manager: Then choosing the larger number would not resolve the business-definition problem.
  4. BI analyst: Correct. We should identify what decision this summary supports and obtain the approved definition from its owner.
  5. Commercial manager: I need an operational comparison, not advice about accounting recognition or what to report externally.
  6. BI analyst: Understood. I'll keep the discussion within the internal metric and refer accounting questions to the appropriate specialist.
  7. Commercial manager: Please ask the owner about refunds, tax treatment, and the time boundary before reconciling the totals.
  8. BI analyst: I will. I'll also retain both source calculations rather than overwrite one without understanding the difference.
  9. Commercial manager: Once the definitions are clear, we can decide whether the reports should use different names.
  10. BI analyst: Exactly. The first outcome is a shared meaning, followed by a comparison using consistent rules.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

Two active-user totals, two different events
  1. Read turns 1-10. Stress qualifying event and denominator as separate choices.
  2. Switch roles for turns 11-20. Keep the subset relationship clear when rejecting addition.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

One count, two possible bases

A month has 1,000 eligible users, 800 distinct login users, and 400 distinct transacting users, all within the login group. The question asks for transaction activity among all eligible users.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Analyst: "The relevant distinct-user count is ___."

Answer and explanation

D. 400

The supplied transaction-active group contains four hundred distinct users.

2. Manager: "The eligible-user denominator is ___."

Answer and explanation

D. 1000

The requested rate uses all one thousand eligible users as its base.

3. Analyst: "The requested rate is ___ percent."

Answer and explanation

B. 40

Four hundred divided by one thousand equals forty percent.

4. Manager: "Using login users instead would give ___ percent."

Answer and explanation

C. 50

Four hundred divided by eight hundred logged-in users equals fifty percent.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

Lesson 01: Metric Definitions and Business Questions
Original fictional case: Analyst Mei and product lead Rafael compare September figures for the same 2,000 eligible users, using UTC calendar boundaries and excluding internal and test accounts. One report counts 1,200 distinct users with a successful login. Another counts 900 distinct users with a completed transaction. All 900 are within the login group. Both queries match their stated definitions. The business question is how many eligible users completed a transaction, not how many signed in. The team must choose an accurate label and rate.
Language workshop: Two active-user totals, two different events
Communication goal: Reconcile apparently conflicting metrics by stating the event, distinct entity, window, and denominator.
Language guidance: 900/2,000 is 45% of eligible users. 900/1,200 is 75% of users who logged in. Both calculations are valid for their stated bases, but only the first answers the supplied eligible-user question.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): "Are we measuring sign-in or completed transactions?" / "Each user is counted once." / "The qualifying event is a completed transaction." / "Use the September calendar month in UTC." / "Both measures exclude internal and test accounts." / "Both queries match their own definitions." / "The reports use different activity events." / "Nine hundred users answer the transaction question." / "The eligible population is 2,000 users." / "That is 45% of eligible users." / "Seventy-five percent uses logged-in users as its base." / "The transaction group is a subset of the login group." / "Call this transaction-active users." / "Keep the login measure under its own label." / "The metric owner should approve the shared definition." / "We need consistent meaning, not an artificial match between different events."
Vocabulary: metric: A defined quantitative measure. / business question: The decision-relevant question an analysis is meant to answer. / metric definition: Rules specifying exactly how a measure is calculated. / qualifying event: An action meeting the condition for inclusion. / distinct user: A unique user counted once under a defined identity rule. / event count: The number of recorded qualifying occurrences. / eligible population: The complete group allowed into the measure's base. / denominator: The total used as the base of a rate. / numerator: The counted outcome placed over the base of a rate. / reporting window: The time period included in a measure. / calendar month: A month bounded by the adopted calendar and time zone. / time zone: The time reference used to assign event dates. / exclusion rule: A condition removing records from the measure. / internal account: An account belonging to the organization's own personnel or systems. / test account: An account used for testing rather than genuine activity. / deduplication: Removing repeated representations of the same counted entity. / identity resolution: Connecting records that refer to the same entity. / active user: A user meeting a specified activity definition. / login-based activity: Activity defined by a qualifying sign-in. / transaction-based activity: Activity defined by a qualifying transaction. / subset: A group entirely contained within another group. / metric owner: The role accountable for a measure's definition and use. / semantic layer: Shared definitions connecting data to business meaning. / reconciliation: Explaining and resolving differences between reported figures.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-10. Stress qualifying event and denominator as separate choices. Switch roles for turns 11-20. Keep the subset relationship clear when rejecting addition. Check the transfer. Read 40% and 50% with their different population bases.

Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Lesson 01: Metric Definitions and Business Questions
Original fictional case: Analyst Mei and product lead Rafael compare September figures for the same 2,000 eligible users, using UTC calendar boundaries and excluding internal and test accounts. One report counts 1,200 distinct users with a successful login. Another counts 900 distinct users with a completed transaction. All 900 are within the login group. Both queries match their stated definitions. The business question is how many eligible users completed a transaction, not how many signed in. The team must choose an accurate label and rate.
Language workshop: Two active-user totals, two different events
Communication goal: Reconcile apparently conflicting metrics by stating the event, distinct entity, window, and denominator.
Language guidance: 900/2,000 is 45% of eligible users. 900/1,200 is 75% of users who logged in. Both calculations are valid for their stated bases, but only the first answers the supplied eligible-user question.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): "Are we measuring sign-in or completed transactions?" / "Each user is counted once." / "The qualifying event is a completed transaction." / "Use the September calendar month in UTC." / "Both measures exclude internal and test accounts." / "Both queries match their own definitions." / "The reports use different activity events." / "Nine hundred users answer the transaction question." / "The eligible population is 2,000 users." / "That is 45% of eligible users." / "Seventy-five percent uses logged-in users as its base." / "The transaction group is a subset of the login group." / "Call this transaction-active users." / "Keep the login measure under its own label." / "The metric owner should approve the shared definition." / "We need consistent meaning, not an artificial match between different events."
Vocabulary: metric: A defined quantitative measure. / business question: The decision-relevant question an analysis is meant to answer. / metric definition: Rules specifying exactly how a measure is calculated. / qualifying event: An action meeting the condition for inclusion. / distinct user: A unique user counted once under a defined identity rule. / event count: The number of recorded qualifying occurrences. / eligible population: The complete group allowed into the measure's base. / denominator: The total used as the base of a rate. / numerator: The counted outcome placed over the base of a rate. / reporting window: The time period included in a measure. / calendar month: A month bounded by the adopted calendar and time zone. / time zone: The time reference used to assign event dates. / exclusion rule: A condition removing records from the measure. / internal account: An account belonging to the organization's own personnel or systems. / test account: An account used for testing rather than genuine activity. / deduplication: Removing repeated representations of the same counted entity. / identity resolution: Connecting records that refer to the same entity. / active user: A user meeting a specified activity definition. / login-based activity: Activity defined by a qualifying sign-in. / transaction-based activity: Activity defined by a qualifying transaction. / subset: A group entirely contained within another group. / metric owner: The role accountable for a measure's definition and use. / semantic layer: Shared definitions connecting data to business meaning. / reconciliation: Explaining and resolving differences between reported figures.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-10. Stress qualifying event and denominator as separate choices. Switch roles for turns 11-20. Keep the subset relationship clear when rejecting addition. Check the transfer. Read 40% and 50% with their different population bases.
Partner: Mei: Data analyst / Rafael: Product lead
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A month has 1,000 eligible users, 800 distinct login users, and 400 distinct transacting users, all within the login group. The question asks for transaction activity among all eligible users.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Rafael: Who signs off if the transaction event changes next month? I do not want the same label to hide a new definition.
Mei: Assign a metric owner and record the approved version. That gives both teams a place to resolve changes before comparing totals that no longer mean the same thing.
Rafael: My summary will be nine hundred transaction-active users, forty-five percent of two thousand eligible users, September UTC. The login measure remains separately labeled.
Mei: That completes the reconciliation. We have preserved both correct calculations, selected the measure that answers the decision, and removed the ambiguity that created the apparent conflict.
END REFERENCE
Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

Professional audience: data analysts, BI developers, analytics engineers, data scientists, reporting teams, business analysts, and data-driven managers
Scope: Original fictional English practice, not statistical certification, legal advice, a privacy determination, or authorization to use customer data. All datasets, thresholds, local approval rules, and business decisions are invented. Actual analysis must follow the applicable definitions, methods, access controls, and responsible review.

02Data Quality and TrustRefreshed today, complete only through Monday+

Your goal

Explain mixed source freshness and set an appropriate reporting boundary without mislabeling missing data as zero.

This lesson in the book, page 13 ↗

A Read the situation

Refreshed today, complete only through Monday

Reporting analyst Asha reviews a dashboard with operations manager Tomas on Wednesday at 09:00 UTC. The dashboard refresh completed, and the orders source is verified complete through Tuesday 23:59 UTC. The refunds source is verified complete only through Monday 23:59 UTC because its next delivery is delayed. A Monday-Tuesday net-order-value figure subtracts available refunds from orders, so the periods are misaligned. Asha can publish a verified Monday-only comparison; Tuesday refunds are unknown, not confirmed zero. No delivery time for the missing source is confirmed.

Asha: Reporting analyst / Tomas: Operations manager

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. Which source is complete only through Monday?

Answer and explanation

A. Refunds

The briefing explicitly limits refund coverage to Monday while orders extend through Tuesday.

2. What is known about Tuesday refunds?

Answer and explanation

D. They are unknown in the available source.

Missing source coverage does not establish the amount of Tuesday refunds.

3. Which comparison can Asha publish as verified?

Answer and explanation

C. Monday-only, using aligned source coverage

Both sources are verified complete through Monday, making that shared period usable.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

A refresh timestamp records when processing ran, not how current every input was. Even a latest event timestamp alone would not prove completeness. Here, the brief explicitly verifies different complete-through cutoffs for the two sources.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
data quality
Fitness of data for its intended use.assess data quality
freshness
How recent data is relative to the intended use.monitor data freshness
timeliness
Availability of data within the required time.define timeliness requirements
completeness
Presence of the required records or values.verify source completeness
accuracy
Agreement between data and the facts it represents.assess data accuracy
validity
Conformance to defined formats or rules.test data validity
consistency
Agreement across related representations or rules.check data consistency
uniqueness
Absence of unintended duplicate records.test record uniqueness
refresh timestamp
The time a report or model was last updated.display the refresh timestamp
source cutoff
The boundary through which source data is available or complete.state the source cutoff
event time
When the represented business event occurred.distinguish event time from load time
load time
When data entered the relevant system.record load time
upstream source
An earlier system supplying data to a pipeline.inspect upstream sources
pipeline
A sequence of data movement and transformation steps.monitor the data pipeline
ingestion delay
A lag in receiving data from a source.investigate ingestion delay
late-arriving data
Records received after the expected processing point.handle late-arriving data
backfill
Loading previously missing historical records.validate a backfill
watermark
A tracked boundary used to process or assess data progress.inspect the pipeline watermark
coverage window
The period or population represented by available data.align coverage windows
reconciliation check
A comparison used to verify agreement between records or totals.run reconciliation checks
null value
A marker for missing or unknown information in a data field.distinguish null from zero
zero value
A recorded quantity of none, not automatically a missing value.preserve zero values correctly
data-quality notice
A visible explanation of a known data limitation.publish a data-quality notice
recovery estimate
A qualified expectation for restoration of a data flow.qualify the recovery estimate

Unknown Tuesday refunds must not be presented as zero refunds. A combined Monday-Tuesday net figure would mix different coverage windows. A verified Monday-only view is a bounded alternative while the missing delivery is unresolved.

C Notice the language

Explain the affected decision, not just the pipeline

A completed refresh can sound like an all-clear to a business reader. Name the source that is late, the measure affected, and the period that remains usable. This gives colleagues a practical reporting option while preserving the visible limitation.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Acknowledge the refresh
"The dashboard refresh completed at 09:00 UTC."
Separate source coverage
"That does not mean every source is current through Tuesday."
Name the lagging source
"Refunds are complete only through Monday."
State the other cutoff
"Orders are verified through Tuesday 23:59 UTC."
Locate the affected measure
"The two-day net figure mixes coverage periods."
Distinguish missing from zero
"Tuesday refunds are unknown, not confirmed zero."
Offer a usable view
"We can publish the verified Monday-only comparison."
Keep the limitation visible
"Place the source-cutoff note beside the figure."
Avoid a false promise
"The missing delivery time is not confirmed."
Ask for the right evidence
"Check source completeness, not only job completion."
Describe the dependency
"The calculation depends on both orders and refunds."
Name the next validation
"Reconcile the backfill before republishing the two-day figure."
Preserve the old status
"Keep the affected view marked incomplete until validation passes."
Clarify timing
"Load time and event time are different."
Bound the current claim
"This view is complete through Monday only."
Close the update
"Publish the aligned period and report the remaining source delay."
6 language and precision notes
Fresh
Relative to a defined required time, not merely a green icon.
Refreshed
Describes processing, not necessarily complete source coverage.
Through Monday
State the actual cutoff and time zone.
Unknown
Must not silently become zero.
Backfilled
Requires validation before the limitation is removed.
Expected
An estimate needing a basis, not a delivery guarantee.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which dashboard note is accurate?

Answer and explanation

A. Refreshed Wednesday 09:00 UTC; refunds complete through Monday; two-day net value incomplete.

The note separates successful processing from source coverage and identifies the affected measure.

2. Why is Monday-only reporting usable here?

Answer and explanation

D. Both sources are verified complete through that shared period.

The relevant fact is aligned verified coverage, not an inherent property of Monday.

3. Which check is insufficient by itself?

Answer and explanation

A. Seeing that the dashboard refresh job completed

A completed refresh can process stale inputs without repairing their missing coverage.

4. What should happen after the delayed records arrive?

Answer and explanation

A. Validate and reconcile them before removing the limitation.

Receiving records is followed by verification, and event dates should preserve business timing.

Check explanations in the book, page 87 ↗

E Conversations

Refreshed today, complete only through Monday

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Asha: Reporting analyst / Tomas: Operations manager

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • recovery estimate
  • backfill
  • source cutoff
  • completeness
  • coverage window
  • data-quality notice
  • timeliness
  • zero value
  • event time
  • refresh timestamp
  1. Tomas
    The dashboard says refreshed this morning. Can I use the Monday-Tuesday net-order-value number in the operations meeting as our complete two-day result?
  2. Asha
    Not yet. The 1 is Wednesday at nine UTC, but the inputs have different coverage. Orders are verified through Tuesday; refunds are complete only through Monday.
  3. Tomas
    I had assumed that a successful refresh meant every table contained the latest business activity. Which boundary should I read instead for the refund data?
  4. Asha
    Read the 2. Refunds are complete through Monday at twenty-three fifty-nine UTC. The next delivery is delayed, so Tuesday is not covered by the available records.
  5. Tomas
    Then the two-day net figure has Tuesday orders but not the corresponding Tuesday refunds. It could look better simply because that input is incomplete.
  6. Asha
    Yes. The 3 is misaligned. We cannot interpret the result as a complete two-day net amount when the subtraction uses refunds from a shorter period.
  7. Tomas
    The chart fills the empty Tuesday cell with zero. Should we change that to unknown while the refund delivery is missing?
  8. Asha
    Missing is not a 4. We do not know the amount yet. Showing zero as the business result would turn a source limitation into a false claim about customer activity.
  9. Tomas
    I still need a reliable number for the meeting. Could we show Monday by itself, since both sources are verified complete through that day?
  10. Asha
    Yes. That respects 5 for the shared period in this case. We can publish the Monday-only comparison and make clear that the two-day figure remains incomplete.
  11. Tomas
    The note should be near the result, not somewhere people will miss it. I will explain that Tuesday orders alone do not settle the net figure.
  12. Asha
    Add a 6 beside the affected view. State which source is delayed, its complete-through time, and which period remains usable rather than labeling the entire dashboard unreliable.
  13. Tomas
    Do we know when the missing refund delivery will arrive? I would like to tell the meeting whether the full figure will be available this afternoon.
  14. Asha
    There is no confirmed 7. I will request an update from the source owner, but I should not invent a delivery time simply because the meeting needs one.
  15. Tomas
    Once the rows arrive, we will have business events from Tuesday loaded on a later day. We should not move those refunds into Wednesday totals.
  16. Asha
    Correct. Preserve 8 separately from load time. The records belong to the appropriate business period even when the pipeline receives them later than expected.
  17. Tomas
    When the delivery arrives, who checks that it is complete? I do not want the warning removed just because the refresh turns green.
  18. Asha
    Validate the 9 and document the checks. A green refresh icon should not be our only evidence that the previously incomplete reporting period is now fit for use.
  19. Tomas
    I will take the Monday-only view to the meeting, explain the Tuesday refund gap, and avoid promising a two-day net result before it is verified.
  20. Asha
    That is the right 10 message: a useful aligned result now, a specific limitation, and a clear validation step before the fuller figure is released.

Answers and explanations
  1. refresh timestamp Turn 2. The refresh timestamp shows when the dashboard processing completed, not the complete-through date of each input.
  2. source cutoff Turn 4. The source cutoff identifies the period through which the refund source is verified complete, here Monday 23:59 UTC.
  3. coverage window Turn 6. The coverage window differs across orders and refunds, making the combined two-day net measure misaligned.
  4. zero value Turn 8. A zero value asserts a quantity of none; absent Tuesday refund coverage does not support that assertion.
  5. completeness Turn 10. Completeness concerns the required records for the stated period; the briefing verifies both sources for Monday.
  6. data-quality notice Turn 12. The data-quality notice makes the source limitation and affected measure visible where readers interpret the result.
  7. recovery estimate Turn 14. A recovery estimate needs a supported expectation; this case supplies no confirmed time for the missing delivery.
  8. event time Turn 16. Event time records when the business event occurred, which remains distinct from the later ingestion or load time.
  9. backfill Turn 18. The backfill loads previously missing records, which need verification before the incomplete-status notice can be removed.
  10. timeliness Turn 20. Timeliness is availability within the required period; the refund delay should be disclosed while the usable aligned view is preserved.

Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

A refreshed dashboard contains Monday's source data

Fictional data-quality update using the lesson case: the dashboard refreshed today, but one source table contains data only through Monday.

  1. Business manager: The dashboard refreshed today. Can I assume every figure includes activity through this morning?
  2. Data analyst: No. One source table only contains data through Monday, despite today's dashboard refresh.
  3. Business manager: Then the display update completed, but the underlying source freshness is different.
  4. Data analyst: Exactly. The pipeline can refresh a report without receiving newer records from every source.
  5. Business manager: Does that prove the Monday data is inaccurate, or only that later activity is missing?
  6. Data analyst: The known issue is the coverage date. We haven't established that the existing records are incorrect.
  7. Business manager: Please make the affected source and cutoff visible so readers don't treat all totals as current.
  8. Data analyst: I'll label that boundary and ask the source owner for the pending update status.
  9. Business manager: Can you promise the missing activity will appear tomorrow if the dashboard runs again?
  10. Data analyst: Not without source confirmation. I'll report the next verified update rather than assume refresh means completeness.
A successful pipeline with a missing region

Fictional reporting handoff: a pipeline run succeeded, but its input manifest contains files from three of four expected regions.

  1. Operations director: The pipeline is green. Does that confirm the regional report is complete for all four regions?
  2. Data engineer: The run succeeded technically, but the input manifest includes only three of the four expected regions.
  3. Operations director: So the success status tells us the available input was processed, not that all input arrived.
  4. Data engineer: Correct. Completeness remains unresolved for the missing region even though the processing step finished.
  5. Operations director: Should its missing values be displayed as zero sales until the file arrives?
  6. Data engineer: No. Missing data isn't evidence of zero activity. We should label that region's figures unavailable.
  7. Operations director: Please request the source status and explain which comparisons are affected by the gap.
  8. Data engineer: I'll contact the regional data owner and retain the input manifest with the update.
  9. Operations director: Then today's report can show completed processing and incomplete coverage without concealing either.
  10. Data engineer: Agreed. I'll confirm when the missing source is received and checked before calling the report complete.
Corrected records have not reached the report

Fictional data-quality case: duplicate source records were corrected, but the dashboard has not rerun since that correction.

  1. Source owner: We corrected the duplicate records. Can you tell users the dashboard totals are fixed now?
  2. BI analyst: The source correction is complete, but the dashboard hasn't rerun since that change.
  3. Source owner: Then the displayed totals may still reflect the earlier records until processing and verification finish.
  4. BI analyst: Exactly. We need to distinguish source repair, pipeline refresh, and checking the resulting report.
  5. Source owner: Could a scheduled refresh be treated as completed remediation because it usually succeeds?
  6. BI analyst: No. A planned run isn't an observed result, and we still need to verify the affected totals.
  7. Source owner: I'll provide the correction record and identify the affected keys through the normal data-quality channel.
  8. BI analyst: Thank you. I'll use that scope to check the refresh outcome without changing unrelated figures.
  9. Source owner: Please keep the user update clear about what has been repaired and what remains pending.
  10. BI analyst: I will. We'll confirm the displayed correction after the run and validation, not before.

A current job can use an old source

A report refreshed Friday. Sales are verified complete through Thursday, but returns only through Wednesday. Thursday returns are unknown. Both sources are complete for Wednesday.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Analyst: "The refresh ran on ___."

Answer and explanation

C. Friday

The briefing explicitly identifies Friday as the processing day.

2. Manager: "Returns are complete only through ___."

Answer and explanation

D. Wednesday

The source cutoff for returns is Wednesday, not the later refresh day.

3. Analyst: "Thursday returns are ___."

Answer and explanation

B. unknown

Missing Thursday coverage does not prove a zero business amount.

4. Manager: "A Wednesday-only comparison is ___."

Answer and explanation

A. aligned

Both sources are verified complete for Wednesday, so their coverage aligns.

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Lesson 02: Data Quality and Trust
Original fictional case: Reporting analyst Asha reviews a dashboard with operations manager Tomas on Wednesday at 09:00 UTC. The dashboard refresh completed, and the orders source is verified complete through Tuesday 23:59 UTC. The refunds source is verified complete only through Monday 23:59 UTC because its next delivery is delayed. A Monday-Tuesday net-order-value figure subtracts available refunds from orders, so the periods are misaligned. Asha can publish a verified Monday-only comparison; Tuesday refunds are unknown, not confirmed zero. No delivery time for the missing source is confirmed.
Language workshop: Refreshed today, complete only through Monday
Communication goal: Explain mixed source freshness and set an appropriate reporting boundary without mislabeling missing data as zero.
Language guidance: A refresh timestamp records when processing ran, not how current every input was. Even a latest event timestamp alone would not prove completeness. Here, the brief explicitly verifies different complete-through cutoffs for the two sources.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): "The dashboard refresh completed at 09:00 UTC." / "That does not mean every source is current through Tuesday." / "Refunds are complete only through Monday." / "Orders are verified through Tuesday 23:59 UTC." / "The two-day net figure mixes coverage periods." / "Tuesday refunds are unknown, not confirmed zero." / "We can publish the verified Monday-only comparison." / "Place the source-cutoff note beside the figure." / "The missing delivery time is not confirmed." / "Check source completeness, not only job completion." / "The calculation depends on both orders and refunds." / "Reconcile the backfill before republishing the two-day figure." / "Keep the affected view marked incomplete until validation passes." / "Load time and event time are different." / "This view is complete through Monday only." / "Publish the aligned period and report the remaining source delay."
Vocabulary: data quality: Fitness of data for its intended use. / freshness: How recent data is relative to the intended use. / timeliness: Availability of data within the required time. / completeness: Presence of the required records or values. / accuracy: Agreement between data and the facts it represents. / validity: Conformance to defined formats or rules. / consistency: Agreement across related representations or rules. / uniqueness: Absence of unintended duplicate records. / refresh timestamp: The time a report or model was last updated. / source cutoff: The boundary through which source data is available or complete. / event time: When the represented business event occurred. / load time: When data entered the relevant system. / upstream source: An earlier system supplying data to a pipeline. / pipeline: A sequence of data movement and transformation steps. / ingestion delay: A lag in receiving data from a source. / late-arriving data: Records received after the expected processing point. / backfill: Loading previously missing historical records. / watermark: A tracked boundary used to process or assess data progress. / coverage window: The period or population represented by available data. / reconciliation check: A comparison used to verify agreement between records or totals. / null value: A marker for missing or unknown information in a data field. / zero value: A recorded quantity of none, not automatically a missing value. / data-quality notice: A visible explanation of a known data limitation. / recovery estimate: A qualified expectation for restoration of a data flow.

Tomas: I will take the Monday-only view to the meeting, explain the Tuesday refund gap, and avoid promising a two-day net result before it is verified.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.

Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.

Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.

Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
