03Dashboards and Executive ReportingFour hundred eighty needs a denominator and a target+

Your goal

Turn an isolated monthly count into a clear performance statement with definition, target, comparison, and units.

This lesson in the book, page 22 ↗

A Read the situation

Four hundred eighty needs a denominator and a target

BI analyst Jonas reviews a card labeled 480 with service director Elena. It counts September requests completed within 24 hours of receipt: 480 of 500 eligible requests. All requests have a complete observation window, and the remaining 20 finished late. The approved target is at least 95%. August had 450 timely completions among 500 eligible requests under the same definition. Jonas must report the current rate, position against target, and change from August without calling a percentage-point change a relative percentage increase.

Jonas: Business-intelligence analyst / Elena: Service director

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What is the September timely-completion rate?

Answer and explanation

A. 96%

Four hundred eighty timely requests divided by five hundred eligible requests equals ninety-six percent.

2. How does September compare with the target?

Answer and explanation

B. One percentage point above the 95% target

Ninety-six percent minus ninety-five percent is one percentage point.

3. What was the August rate?

Answer and explanation

D. 90%

Four hundred fifty timely requests divided by five hundred eligible requests equals ninety percent.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

September is 96%: 480/500. August is 90%: 450/500. The increase is 6 percentage points, or approximately 6.67% relative to August. September exceeds the 95% target by 1 percentage point.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
KPI
Key performance indicator, a measure tied to an objective.define a KPI
dashboard card
A compact display of a selected metric.label the dashboard card
performance target
A specified desired level of a measure.compare with the performance target
actual result
The observed value of the defined measure.report the actual result
comparison period
An earlier or alternative interval used as a reference.state the comparison period
variance
A difference from a specified reference value.explain the target variance
percentage point
One unit of difference between percentage values.report percentage-point changes
relative change
Change divided by the original reference value.calculate relative change
month-on-month
Compared with the immediately preceding month.describe month-on-month movement
year-on-year
Compared with the corresponding period a year earlier.report year-on-year change
service-level target
An agreed performance threshold for a service measure.track the service-level target
timely completion
Completion within the specified permitted duration.measure timely completion
elapsed time
Duration between defined start and end events.calculate elapsed time
observation window
The period available to observe the relevant outcome.complete the observation window
threshold
A boundary used for a rule or decision.apply the threshold
direction of improvement
Whether higher or lower values are favorable.state the direction of improvement
trend
A pattern across a sequence of observations.qualify a trend claim
seasonality
Recurring variation related to calendar patterns.assess seasonality
run chart
A display of values ordered through time.review the run chart
annotation
Explanatory text attached to a chart or figure.add a relevant annotation
drill-down
Moving from a summary to more detailed data.provide a drill-down
exception queue
Items selected for attention under a defined rule.review the exception queue
comparability
The extent to which measures use compatible bases.establish period comparability
executive summary
A concise account of the main evidence and decision relevance.sharpen the executive summary

The 480 card alone omits its denominator, timing rule, target, and comparison. Two monthly results establish a month-on-month improvement here, not a sustained long-term trend or an explanation of why performance changed.

C Notice the language

Make the interpretation portable

A useful executive card should survive being quoted outside the meeting. Include the measure and comparison in a sentence that can be repeated accurately. Avoid forcing the listener to infer whether a larger number represents improvement, demand growth, or a changed definition.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Name the measure
"This is the share completed within 24 hours."
State the start
"The clock starts when the request is received."
Give count and base
"September recorded 480 timely completions out of 500."
State the result
"The timely-completion rate is 96%."
Compare with target
"That is one percentage point above the 95% target."
Give the prior result
"August was 450 of 500, or 90%."
Use the correct change unit
"The rate rose by six percentage points."
Give the relative alternative
"That is about a 6.67% relative increase."
Protect comparability
"Both months use the same definition."
Confirm maturity
"Every request has a complete observation window."
Keep the exceptions visible
"Twenty September requests finished late."
Avoid an invented cause
"These totals do not explain the improvement."
Bound the trend
"This is a two-month comparison, not a sustained-trend claim."
Make the card interpretable
"Add the definition, denominator, target, and comparison."
Offer the next view
"The drill-down can show the late requests."
Close the readout
"September met the target and improved from August on the same basis."
6 language and precision notes
Above target
State the amount and use the correct unit.
Up six points
Not identical to up six percent.
Within 24 hours
Requires defined start and completion events.
Monthly total
Does not by itself reveal a performance rate.
Trend
Needs more than a confident adjective about two observations.
Favorable
Depends on the measure's direction and target.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which card text is most complete?

Answer and explanation

C. September: 480/500 within 24 hours, 96%; target 95%; August 90%.

The complete text provides the definition, count, denominator, target, and comparable prior rate.

2. Which change statement is accurate?

Answer and explanation

D. Up 6 percentage points from August

Ninety-six percent minus ninety percent is six percentage points.

3. What is the approximate relative increase from 90% to 96%?

Answer and explanation

D. 6.67%

The six-point difference divided by the ninety-percent reference rate is approximately 6.67%.

4. What cannot be concluded from the supplied totals?

Answer and explanation

B. The specific cause of the improvement

The briefing supplies outcomes and comparable definitions but no causal investigation evidence.

Check explanations in the book, page 89 ↗

E Conversations

Four hundred eighty needs a denominator and a target

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Jonas: Business-intelligence analyst / Elena: Service director

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • comparison period
  • relative change
  • KPI
  • executive summary
  • comparability
  • drill-down
  • actual result
  • percentage point
  • month-on-month
  • observation window
  1. Elena
    The card says four hundred eighty with a green arrow. What does it count, and how should I interpret it?
  2. Jonas
    The 1 is timely completion: requests finished within twenty-four hours of receipt. Four hundred eighty is the timely count, not the rate or the total demand.
  3. Elena
    There were five hundred eligible requests in September, and the other twenty finished late. All requests have had enough time for us to observe their outcomes.
  4. Jonas
    Then the 2 is ninety-six percent, or four hundred eighty out of five hundred. We should display both the count and the base so the rate is interpretable.
  5. Elena
    Our approved target is at least ninety-five percent. That means September passed, but I want the amount above the target described accurately.
  6. Jonas
    It is one 3 above target. Saying one percent without qualification can suggest a relative calculation, which is a different way of expressing the comparison.
  7. Elena
    August had four hundred fifty timely requests out of five hundred. The same definition and exclusions apply, so that earlier rate was ninety percent.
  8. Jonas
    August is the 4. September increased by six percentage points on that basis. We should name the month rather than leave the audience guessing what the arrow compares.
  9. Elena
    My draft says up six percent. Should I change that to six percentage points? I need wording I can repeat without changing the calculation.
  10. Jonas
    Correct it. The 5 is approximately six point six seven percent. The absolute difference between the percentage values is six points. Both are valid when labeled, but they are not interchangeable.
  11. Elena
    I also want to avoid the green arrow hiding the twenty late requests. Meeting the overall threshold does not mean every customer received the target service.
  12. Jonas
    We can provide a 6 for those late cases. The headline can report that the target was met while the detail preserves the exceptions that still need operational attention.
  13. Elena
    Can I call it a sustained improvement, or do two months only support the month-on-month comparison? I want to recognize the result accurately.
  14. Jonas
    We can report the 7 improvement. Two months do not establish a sustained trend, and these totals do not identify the cause. Recognition does not require a stronger statistical story.
  15. Elena
    Fair. We can acknowledge the result without attributing it to a particular staffing change that has not been evaluated. The definition should stay visible too.
  16. Jonas
    Preserve 8 by retaining the same start, completion rule, exclusions, and denominator. A future definition change should be flagged instead of being presented as an unexplained performance movement.
  17. Elena
    The clock begins on receipt and stops on completion. We should also state that the data covers fully observed requests, not a partial month of immature outcomes.
  18. Jonas
    Yes, the 9 is complete for every request here. That matters because unresolved timing can otherwise distort a rate even when the arithmetic itself is correct.
  19. Elena
    September was ninety-six percent: one point above target, six above August, twenty requests late. The cause is unestablished.
  20. Jonas
    That is a useful 10. The card will include the definition and base, and the detail will support the late-case review rather than asking the audience to infer everything from 480.

Answers and explanations
  1. KPI Turn 2. The KPI is the defined performance measure, here the share of eligible requests completed within twenty-four hours.
  2. actual result Turn 4. The actual result is ninety-six percent, calculated from 480 timely completions among 500 eligible requests.
  3. percentage point Turn 6. A percentage point expresses the difference between percentage values, so 96% exceeds 95% by one point.
  4. comparison period Turn 8. The comparison period is the earlier month, whose ninety-percent rate provides the reference for the reported improvement.
  5. relative change Turn 10. Relative change divides the six-point rise by the ninety-percent August rate, giving approximately 6.67%.
  6. drill-down Turn 12. A drill-down exposes the individual or grouped exceptions beneath the favorable summary rate.
  7. month-on-month Turn 14. Month-on-month describes the immediate September-versus-August comparison without claiming a longer established pattern.
  8. comparability Turn 16. Comparability rests on the shared definition, eligibility, and observation basis that make the two monthly rates meaningful to compare.
  9. observation window Turn 18. The observation window must allow the outcome to be observed; the briefing confirms all requests have complete windows.
  10. executive summary Turn 20. The executive summary brings together the result, target, comparison, exceptions, and limits in a concise repeatable account.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Elena: The card says four hundred eighty with a green arrow. What does it count, and how should I interpret it?
  2. Jonas: The KPI is timely completion: requests finished within twenty-four hours of receipt. Four hundred eighty is the timely count, not the rate or the total demand.
  3. Elena: There were five hundred eligible requests in September, and the other twenty finished late. All requests have had enough time for us to observe their outcomes.
  4. Jonas: Then the actual result is ninety-six percent, or four hundred eighty out of five hundred. We should display both the count and the base so the rate is interpretable.
  5. Elena: Our approved target is at least ninety-five percent. That means September passed, but I want the amount above the target described accurately.
  6. Jonas: It is one percentage point above target. Saying one percent without qualification can suggest a relative calculation, which is a different way of expressing the comparison.
  7. Elena: August had four hundred fifty timely requests out of five hundred. The same definition and exclusions apply, so that earlier rate was ninety percent.
  8. Jonas: August is the comparison period. September increased by six percentage points on that basis. We should name the month rather than leave the audience guessing what the arrow compares.
  9. Elena: My draft says up six percent. Should I change that to six percentage points? I need wording I can repeat without changing the calculation.
  10. Jonas: Correct it. The relative change is approximately six point six seven percent. The absolute difference between the percentage values is six points. Both are valid when labeled, but they are not interchangeable.
  11. Elena: I also want to avoid the green arrow hiding the twenty late requests. Meeting the overall threshold does not mean every customer received the target service.
  12. Jonas: We can provide a drill-down for those late cases. The headline can report that the target was met while the detail preserves the exceptions that still need operational attention.
  13. Elena: Can I call it a sustained improvement, or do two months only support the month-on-month comparison? I want to recognize the result accurately.
  14. Jonas: We can report the month-on-month improvement. Two months do not establish a sustained trend, and these totals do not identify the cause. Recognition does not require a stronger statistical story.
  15. Elena: Fair. We can acknowledge the result without attributing it to a particular staffing change that has not been evaluated. The definition should stay visible too.
  16. Jonas: Preserve comparability by retaining the same start, completion rule, exclusions, and denominator. A future definition change should be flagged instead of being presented as an unexplained performance movement.
  17. Elena: The clock begins on receipt and stops on completion. We should also state that the data covers fully observed requests, not a partial month of immature outcomes.
  18. Jonas: Yes, the observation window is complete for every request here. That matters because unresolved timing can otherwise distort a rate even when the arithmetic itself is correct.
  19. Elena: September was ninety-six percent: one point above target, six above August, twenty requests late. The cause is unestablished.
  20. Jonas: That is a useful executive summary. The card will include the definition and base, and the detail will support the late-case review rather than asking the audience to infer everything from 480.
Dialogue in the book, page 28 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

A monthly total without an interpretation frame

Fictional dashboard review using the lesson case: a KPI card has a monthly total but no target, comparison period, or definition.

  1. Executive reader: This monthly total looks large. Is that a good result for the business?
  2. Dashboard designer: The card doesn't provide enough context. We need the metric definition, relevant comparison, and any agreed target.
  3. Executive reader: Could you color it green so readers know what conclusion to draw immediately?
  4. Dashboard designer: Not until a favorable threshold is established. A color shouldn't invent an assessment the evidence doesn't support.
  5. Executive reader: Please explain the difference between a number and a key performance indicator, or KPI.
  6. Dashboard designer: A KPI connects a defined measure to a performance question; the isolated total doesn't show that connection.
  7. Executive reader: Would a drill-down solve the missing definition if readers click into the detailed records?
  8. Dashboard designer: Detail can help investigation, but the overview still needs enough context to interpret its headline.
  9. Executive reader: I'll ask the metric owner for the target and comparison basis rather than guess them.
  10. Dashboard designer: I'll update the card once those are confirmed and show any missing context explicitly meanwhile.
A target is not an alert threshold

Fictional service dashboard: an agreed monthly target exists, but the team has not defined an operational alert threshold.

  1. Service manager: We have a monthly target. Can the dashboard send an alert whenever a daily result misses it?
  2. BI developer: A monthly target and a daily alert threshold answer different questions. We haven't defined that daily rule.
  3. Service manager: Could you explain the distinction without implying we should ignore daily performance completely?
  4. BI developer: The target describes a desired monthly outcome; an alert rule identifies conditions that need timely attention.
  5. Service manager: Then we need a reviewed daily interpretation rather than apply the monthly number to every day.
  6. BI developer: Exactly. Otherwise the dashboard may signal a problem based on an inappropriate comparison period.
  7. Service manager: I'll ask the operational owner what daily condition should trigger investigation and what context matters.
  8. BI developer: Please include the intended response, so the threshold supports an action rather than just another notification.
  9. Service manager: Until then, show daily data without presenting it as an approved pass-or-fail judgment.
  10. BI developer: Agreed. We can keep the monthly KPI and the proposed daily alert clearly separate.
A total can conceal where a service problem occurs

Fictional service report: an overall delay measure is available, but regional breakdowns have not been reviewed.

  1. Operations director: The overall delay measure is stable. Can I say every region is performing consistently?
  2. BI analyst: The total doesn't establish that. Regional differences could be hidden, and we haven't reviewed the breakdown.
  3. Operations director: So a drill-down lets us investigate variation, rather than assume the headline applies everywhere.
  4. BI analyst: Correct. It adds detail beneath the dashboard summary while retaining the same metric definition.
  5. Operations director: Should we label one region as the problem area before we open the underlying view?
  6. BI analyst: No. We don't have that evidence yet. The next step is inspection, not a regional conclusion.
  7. Operations director: Please provide a regional view with comparable periods and clearly identified missing data.
  8. BI analyst: I'll check the coverage and denominator so the drill-down doesn't introduce misleading comparisons.
  9. Operations director: Then the executive headline can state the overall result and note that regional analysis is pending.
  10. BI analyst: Yes. It will distinguish what the summary shows from what the detailed view still needs to establish.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

Four hundred eighty needs a denominator and a target
  1. Read turns 1-10. Say percentage points and relative percent distinctly.
  2. Switch roles for turns 11-20. Keep the twenty late requests in the summary.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Above target and above last month are different

A service completes 180 of 200 eligible requests on time this month. Last month was 170 of 200. The target is 88%, and both months use the same fully observed definition.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Analyst: "This month is ___ percent."

Answer and explanation

A. 90

One hundred eighty divided by two hundred equals ninety percent.

2. Manager: "Last month was ___ percent."

Answer and explanation

D. 85

One hundred seventy divided by two hundred equals eighty-five percent.

3. Analyst: "The month-on-month increase is ___ percentage points."

Answer and explanation

A. five

Ninety percent minus eighty-five percent is five percentage points.

4. Manager: "This month exceeds target by ___ percentage points."

Answer and explanation

B. two

Ninety percent minus the eighty-eight-percent target equals two percentage points.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

04Causation, Correlation, and CaveatsTraining attendees renewed more often+

Your goal

Report a meaningful association while separating the observed difference from an unestablished causal effect.

This lesson in the book, page 31 ↗

A Read the situation

Training attendees renewed more often

Analyst Priya reviews voluntary customer-training results with customer-success lead Marcus. Over the same renewal window, 90 of 100 eligible attendees renewed, compared with 160 of 200 eligible non-attendees. Attendance was not randomized. Baseline motivation and engagement may differ between the groups and were not measured. The records establish a 90% versus 80% renewal association, not the effect of making everyone attend. Priya recommends a reviewed evaluation design before the team uses the difference as a causal benefit claim.

Priya: Data analyst / Marcus: Customer-success lead

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What is the attendee renewal rate?

Answer and explanation

D. 90%

Ninety renewals divided by one hundred eligible attendees equals ninety percent.

2. What is the non-attendee renewal rate?

Answer and explanation

C. 80%

One hundred sixty renewals divided by two hundred eligible non-attendees equals eighty percent.

3. Why is the causal effect not established?

Answer and explanation

B. Attendance was voluntary, and unmeasured baseline differences may matter.

Voluntary selection and possible unmeasured differences prevent the observed comparison from isolating the training effect.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

The observed difference is 10 percentage points: 90%-80%. Relative to the non-attendee rate, it is 12.5%. Neither expression turns the voluntary comparison into an estimated causal benefit for mandatory training.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
association
An observed relationship between variables or groups.report an association
correlation
A measure or description of co-variation.distinguish correlation from causation
causal effect
A change attributable to a specified intervention.estimate a causal effect
confounding
Distortion from factors related to both exposure and outcome.assess potential confounding
self-selection
Participation determined partly by people's own choices.account for self-selection
selection bias
Systematic distortion related to how observations enter groups.evaluate selection bias
baseline characteristic
A feature measured before the exposure or intervention.compare baseline characteristics
motivation
A person's underlying inclination to act or engage.assess baseline motivation
engagement
Participation or involvement under a specified definition.define customer engagement
observational study
Analysis of naturally occurring exposures without assigned treatment.interpret an observational study
random assignment
Allocation to groups by a defined random process.use random assignment
treatment group
The group assigned or exposed to the studied intervention.define the treatment group
comparison group
The group used as a reference for the outcome.select a comparison group
counterfactual
The outcome that would occur under an alternative exposure.define the counterfactual question
renewal
Continuation of an eligible customer contract or relationship.measure customer renewal
renewal window
The period over which renewal outcomes are observed.align renewal windows
eligible customer
A customer meeting the defined inclusion conditions.identify eligible customers
absolute difference
A difference expressed in the original measure's units.state the absolute difference
relative lift
A difference expressed relative to a comparison value.qualify relative lift
adjustment
Accounting for specified variables in an analysis.assess the adjustment method
unmeasured factor
A relevant characteristic not recorded in the data.disclose unmeasured factors
generalizability
Applicability of a result beyond the studied group.assess generalizability
causal claim
A statement that an intervention produced an outcome.qualify a causal claim
evaluation design
The planned method for assessing a program or intervention.review the evaluation design

Unmeasured motivation is a possible confounder, not a proven explanation of the full difference. Likewise, failure to establish causation does not prove that training has no effect. The appropriate conclusion is narrower than either claim.

C Notice the language

Protect the useful observation while correcting the claim

A causal caveat should not sound like all the work was pointless. State the real association first, then explain the specific competing explanation. This helps a colleague keep the finding while replacing an overconfident promise with an appropriate next evaluation.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
State the observed rates
"Attendees renewed at 90%; non-attendees at 80%."
Give the counts
"That is 90 of 100 versus 160 of 200."
Name the difference
"The observed gap is ten percentage points."
Label the finding
"This is an association in the supplied data."
Identify selection
"Customers chose whether to attend."
Name the alternative explanation
"Baseline engagement may differ between the groups."
Preserve uncertainty
"Those baseline factors were not measured."
Reject the causal leap
"We cannot attribute the whole difference to training."
Avoid the opposite overclaim
"This does not prove that training has no effect."
Clarify the counterfactual
"We need to know what would have happened to comparable customers without training."
Keep the window aligned
"Both rates use the same renewal window."
Bound the audience
"The result concerns these eligible customer groups."
Qualify adjustment
"Adjusting measured factors would not automatically remove every unmeasured difference."
Recommend evaluation
"Review a design that can better isolate the training effect."
Revise the headline
"Use higher observed renewal among attendees."
Close the discussion
"Keep the association and remove the unsupported promise."
6 language and precision notes
Because
Introduces a causal claim that needs appropriate support.
Among
Describes a group comparison without asserting cause.
May differ
Identifies a plausible concern, not a proven explanation.
Lift
Specify whether descriptive or causal, relative or absolute.
No proof of effect
Not equivalent to proof of no effect.
Everyone
Extends the claim beyond the observed voluntary groups.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which headline is supported?

Answer and explanation

B. Higher observed renewal among voluntary training attendees

The supported headline describes the observed association without asserting an unestablished causal result.

2. What is the relative observed difference compared with 80%?

Answer and explanation

A. 12.5%

The ten-point gap divided by the eighty-percent reference rate equals 12.5%.

3. Which statement about motivation is accurate?

Answer and explanation

D. It is an unmeasured possible source of group differences.

The briefing identifies motivation as a possible difference but supplies no measurement or randomization.

4. What should a revised recommendation do?

Answer and explanation

A. Preserve the association and seek a reviewed design for a causal evaluation.

A better evaluation can address the causal question while the current association remains honestly reported.

E Conversations

Training attendees renewed more often

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Priya: Data analyst / Marcus: Customer-success lead

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • self-selection
  • unmeasured factor
  • adjustment
  • relative lift
  • generalizability
  • evaluation design
  • causal effect
  • association
  • counterfactual
  • Confounding
  1. Marcus
    Training attendees renewed at ninety percent, versus eighty percent for non-attendees. Can we say the sessions improved renewal by ten points?
  2. Priya
    We can report the 1, but not that causal conclusion from these records. Ninety of one hundred attendees renewed; one hundred sixty of two hundred non-attendees renewed.
  3. Marcus
    Both groups had the same renewal window and eligibility rules. That makes the comparison cleaner, even though attendance was voluntary rather than assigned.
  4. Priya
    Correct, but 2 remains important. Customers who chose training may have been more motivated or engaged already. Those baseline differences were not measured in the supplied data.
  5. Marcus
    Then the ten-point difference could reflect training, pre-existing differences, or both. We should not assume the entire gap was created by the sessions.
  6. Priya
    Exactly. The 3 asks what the intervention changes compared with what would otherwise have happened. This comparison does not isolate that quantity simply because the rates are clearly different.
  7. Marcus
    I do not want the caveat to erase the positive finding. The team has real evidence that attendees renewed more often in this group.
  8. Priya
    Keep that observation. 4 is a reason to qualify the causal interpretation, not to deny the counts. We can state the association and the specific limitation together.
  9. Marcus
    Sales will ask whether you are saying motivation caused the whole gap. We did not measure it, so that is not our conclusion either, is it?
  10. Priya
    Right. It is an 5 that may matter, not a proven explanation of every percentage point. We should avoid replacing one unsupported causal story with another equally unsupported story.
  11. Marcus
    For the numerical wording, ninety minus eighty is ten percentage points. Would a twelve-point-five-percent improvement headline simply express the same observed difference another way?
  12. Priya
    It expresses 6 against the eighty-percent reference rate. But improvement may still imply cause. Say a twelve-point-five-percent higher observed rate, and keep the nonrandom attendance qualification nearby.
  13. Marcus
    The business question is whether offering or requiring training would improve renewal for eligible customers. That is different from describing people who already chose to attend.
  14. Priya
    Yes. The 7 question is what comparable customers would do without the intervention. We need a reviewed evaluation approach suited to that decision, not just a more persuasive headline.
  15. Marcus
    We may be able to add customer size and tenure. Would adjusting for those settle the concern, or would important differences still be missing?
  16. Priya
    A suitable 8 may help with measured differences, but it does not automatically address unmeasured motivation or every source of bias. The method and assumptions need explicit review.
  17. Marcus
    And a mandatory program could reach people unlike the voluntary attendees. Even a stronger estimate in this group might not transfer unchanged to everyone.
  18. Priya
    That is a 9 issue as well as a causal one. The population, intervention, and implementation matter when translating evidence into a wider operational recommendation.
  19. Marcus
    I will revise the slide to higher observed renewal among attendees, show both counts and rates, and recommend a better evaluation before claiming a guaranteed benefit.
  20. Priya
    Good. A reviewed 10 can address the decision we actually face. We have kept the useful finding, corrected its interpretation, and made the next evidence step clear.

Answers and explanations
  1. association Turn 2. The association is the observed difference between voluntary attendance groups, not an isolated effect of training.
  2. self-selection Turn 4. Self-selection means customers chose attendance, which can create relevant differences between the compared groups.
  3. causal effect Turn 6. The causal effect concerns what training itself changes, which the voluntary comparison does not isolate.
  4. Confounding Turn 8. Confounding is a possible distortion from factors related to both attendance and renewal, not proof that the observed association is meaningless.
  5. unmeasured factor Turn 10. Motivation is an unmeasured factor that could matter, but the case does not establish its size or explanatory contribution.
  6. relative lift Turn 12. Relative lift divides the ten-point observed difference by the eighty-percent comparison rate, giving 12.5% without establishing causation.
  7. counterfactual Turn 14. The counterfactual concerns what the relevant customers would have done under a different training exposure, which is not directly observed here.
  8. adjustment Turn 16. Adjustment can account for specified measured variables but does not automatically remove all unmeasured differences or prove a causal effect.
  9. generalizability Turn 18. Generalizability asks whether a result applies beyond the studied voluntary groups to the broader proposed audience.
  10. evaluation design Turn 20. The evaluation design specifies how the next assessment will address the causal question more credibly than the current voluntary comparison.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Marcus: Training attendees renewed at ninety percent, versus eighty percent for non-attendees. Can we say the sessions improved renewal by ten points?
  2. Priya: We can report the association, but not that causal conclusion from these records. Ninety of one hundred attendees renewed; one hundred sixty of two hundred non-attendees renewed.
  3. Marcus: Both groups had the same renewal window and eligibility rules. That makes the comparison cleaner, even though attendance was voluntary rather than assigned.
  4. Priya: Correct, but self-selection remains important. Customers who chose training may have been more motivated or engaged already. Those baseline differences were not measured in the supplied data.
  5. Marcus: Then the ten-point difference could reflect training, pre-existing differences, or both. We should not assume the entire gap was created by the sessions.
  6. Priya: Exactly. The causal effect asks what the intervention changes compared with what would otherwise have happened. This comparison does not isolate that quantity simply because the rates are clearly different.
  7. Marcus: I do not want the caveat to erase the positive finding. The team has real evidence that attendees renewed more often in this group.
  8. Priya: Keep that observation. Confounding is a reason to qualify the causal interpretation, not to deny the counts. We can state the association and the specific limitation together.
  9. Marcus: Sales will ask whether you are saying motivation caused the whole gap. We did not measure it, so that is not our conclusion either, is it?
  10. Priya: Right. It is an unmeasured factor that may matter, not a proven explanation of every percentage point. We should avoid replacing one unsupported causal story with another equally unsupported story.
  11. Marcus: For the numerical wording, ninety minus eighty is ten percentage points. Would a twelve-point-five-percent improvement headline simply express the same observed difference another way?
  12. Priya: It expresses relative lift against the eighty-percent reference rate. But improvement may still imply cause. Say a twelve-point-five-percent higher observed rate, and keep the nonrandom attendance qualification nearby.
  13. Marcus: The business question is whether offering or requiring training would improve renewal for eligible customers. That is different from describing people who already chose to attend.
  14. Priya: Yes. The counterfactual question is what comparable customers would do without the intervention. We need a reviewed evaluation approach suited to that decision, not just a more persuasive headline.
  15. Marcus: We may be able to add customer size and tenure. Would adjusting for those settle the concern, or would important differences still be missing?

Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Training attendance and renewal are associated

Fictional analysis discussion using the lesson case: training attendees renew more often, but attendance is voluntary and motivation may differ.

  1. Customer-success director: Training attendees renew more often. Can we say training caused the higher renewal rate?
  2. Analytics lead: The association is observed, but voluntary attendance creates a selection-bias concern about who chooses training.
  3. Customer-success director: You mean motivated customers might both attend and renew, even without a training effect?
  4. Analytics lead: That is one possible confounder, not a measured explanation we have confirmed in this case.
  5. Customer-success director: So correlation describes the pattern, while causation would require stronger support for the effect of attendance.
  6. Analytics lead: Exactly. We should not treat the group difference as an isolated training effect.
  7. Customer-success director: Can we still use the finding to propose further investigation rather than dismiss it as useless?
  8. Analytics lead: Yes. It raises a useful question and can inform a better-designed evaluation with appropriate review.
  9. Customer-success director: I'll describe attendees' higher renewal as an association and keep the motivation hypothesis explicitly tentative.
  10. Analytics lead: I'll bring the group definitions and limits so the recommendation doesn't promise an unestablished causal benefit.
Service time fell after two operational changes

Fictional operations analysis: average service time fell after both a staffing change and a new intake form; no separate effects were estimated.

  1. Operations manager: Service time fell after the new intake form. Can we credit the form for the improvement?
  2. Data analyst: Staffing changed during the same period, so the before-and-after comparison doesn't isolate the form's effect.
  3. Operations manager: Then the timing supports a question, but it doesn't rule out another explanation for the change.
  4. Data analyst: Correct. The staffing change is a potential confounder in this comparison, not a confirmed sole cause.
  5. Operations manager: Would saying staffing caused it instead be equally unsupported by the current evidence?
  6. Data analyst: Yes. We should describe the observed decrease and both concurrent changes without assigning causation.
  7. Operations manager: What can I recommend now without pretending the analysis has answered which change mattered?
  8. Data analyst: A focused investigation of comparable periods and available process data, with remaining limitations stated.
  9. Operations manager: I'll keep the operational result visible and replace the causal headline with a bounded description.
  10. Data analyst: I'll outline the evidence needed to examine the explanations rather than choose one by narrative appeal.
A survey describes respondents, not every customer

Fictional survey review: satisfied customers responded more often in a voluntary follow-up; nonrespondents' experiences are unknown.

  1. Experience manager: The voluntary follow-up looks positive. Can we say every customer group is satisfied with the service?
  2. Research analyst: The responses describe respondents. Nonrespondents' experiences are unknown, and participation may be related to satisfaction.
  3. Experience manager: We know satisfied customers responded more often here, so selection bias affects the broad interpretation.
  4. Research analyst: Yes. We should state that pattern without inventing what every nonrespondent would have said.
  5. Experience manager: Could we simply assign negative scores to missing responses to compensate for the bias?
  6. Research analyst: No. That would replace missing evidence with another unsupported assumption rather than solve the sampling problem.
  7. Experience manager: Then our result should report the respondent population and avoid a universal customer claim.
  8. Research analyst: Exactly. We can discuss further outreach or another evaluation approach without promising complete representativeness.
  9. Experience manager: I'll make the response boundary explicit and ask what additional evidence would inform the decision.
  10. Research analyst: I'll bring the participation pattern and limitations so the finding remains useful but appropriately bounded.

A difference does not supply its cause

In a voluntary program, 45 of 50 attendees renew and 80 of 100 non-attendees renew. Baseline engagement is unmeasured. Attendance was not randomized.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Analyst: "Attendee renewal is ___ percent."

Answer and explanation

C. 90

Forty-five divided by fifty equals ninety percent for attendees.

2. Lead: "Non-attendee renewal is ___ percent."

Answer and explanation

D. 80

Eighty divided by one hundred equals eighty percent for non-attendees.

3. Analyst: "The observed gap is ___ percentage points."

Answer and explanation

A. ten

Ninety percent minus eighty percent equals ten percentage points.

4. Lead: "The causal effect remains ___."

Answer and explanation

C. unestablished

Voluntary attendance and unmeasured baseline differences prevent this comparison from isolating causation.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Lesson 04: Causation, Correlation, and Caveats
Original fictional case: Analyst Priya reviews voluntary customer-training results with customer-success lead Marcus. Over the same renewal window, 90 of 100 eligible attendees renewed, compared with 160 of 200 eligible non-attendees. Attendance was not randomized. Baseline motivation and engagement may differ between the groups and were not measured. The records establish a 90% versus 80% renewal association, not the effect of making everyone attend. Priya recommends a reviewed evaluation design before the team uses the difference as a causal benefit claim.
Language workshop: Training attendees renewed more often
Communication goal: Report a meaningful association while separating the observed difference from an unestablished causal effect.
Language guidance: The observed difference is 10 percentage points: 90%-80%. Relative to the non-attendee rate, it is 12.5%. Neither expression turns the voluntary comparison into an estimated causal benefit for mandatory training.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): "Attendees renewed at 90%; non-attendees at 80%." / "That is 90 of 100 versus 160 of 200." / "The observed gap is ten percentage points." / "This is an association in the supplied data." / "Customers chose whether to attend." / "Baseline engagement may differ between the groups." / "Those baseline factors were not measured." / "We cannot attribute the whole difference to training." / "This does not prove that training has no effect." / "We need to know what would have happened to comparable customers without training." / "Both rates use the same renewal window." / "The result concerns these eligible customer groups." / "Adjusting measured factors would not automatically remove every unmeasured difference." / "Review a design that can better isolate the training effect." / "Use higher observed renewal among attendees." / "Keep the association and remove the unsupported promise."
Vocabulary: association: An observed relationship between variables or groups. / correlation: A measure or description of co-variation. / causal effect: A change attributable to a specified intervention. / confounding: Distortion from factors related to both exposure and outcome. / self-selection: Participation determined partly by people's own choices. / selection bias: Systematic distortion related to how observations enter groups. / baseline characteristic: A feature measured before the exposure or intervention. / motivation: A person's underlying inclination to act or engage. / engagement: Participation or involvement under a specified definition. / observational study: Analysis of naturally occurring exposures without assigned treatment. / random assignment: Allocation to groups by a defined random process. / treatment group: The group assigned or exposed to the studied intervention. / comparison group: The group used as a reference for the outcome. / counterfactual: The outcome that would occur under an alternative exposure. / renewal: Continuation of an eligible customer contract or relationship. / renewal window: The period over which renewal outcomes are observed. / eligible customer: A customer meeting the defined inclusion conditions. / absolute difference: A difference expressed in the original measure's units. / relative lift: A difference expressed relative to a comparison value. / adjustment: Accounting for specified variables in an analysis. / unmeasured factor: A relevant characteristic not recorded in the data. / generalizability: Applicability of a result beyond the studied group. / causal claim: A statement that an intervention produced an outcome. / evaluation design: The planned method for assessing a program or intervention.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-10. Stress observed association and voluntary attendance. Switch roles for turns 11-20. Keep a possible confounder distinct from a proven explanation. Check the transfer. Read both group rates without attributing the gap to training.
Partner: Priya: Data analyst / Marcus: Customer-success lead
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: In a voluntary program, 45 of 50 attendees renew and 80 of 100 non-attendees renew. Baseline engagement is unmeasured. Attendance was not randomized.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Marcus: And a mandatory program could reach people unlike the voluntary attendees. Even a stronger estimate in this group might not transfer unchanged to everyone.
Priya: That is a generalizability issue as well as a causal one. The population, intervention, and implementation matter when translating evidence into a wider operational recommendation.
Marcus: I will revise the slide to higher observed renewal among attendees, show both counts and rates, and recommend a better evaluation before claiming a guaranteed benefit.
Priya: Good. A reviewed evaluation design can address the decision we actually face. We have kept the useful finding, corrected its interpretation, and made the next evidence step clear.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Course: Data Analytics and Business Intelligence English

Lesson 04: Causation, Correlation, and Caveats
Original fictional case: Analyst Priya reviews voluntary customer-training results with customer-success lead Marcus. Over the same renewal window, 90 of 100 eligible attendees renewed, compared with 160 of 200 eligible non-attendees. Attendance was not randomized. Baseline motivation and engagement may differ between the groups and were not measured. The records establish a 90% versus 80% renewal association, not the effect of making everyone attend. Priya recommends a reviewed evaluation design before the team uses the difference as a causal benefit claim.
Language workshop: Training attendees renewed more often
Communication goal: Report a meaningful association while separating the observed difference from an unestablished causal effect.
Language guidance: The observed difference is 10 percentage points: 90%-80%. Relative to the non-attendee rate, it is 12.5%. Neither expression turns the voluntary comparison into an estimated causal benefit for mandatory training.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): "Attendees renewed at 90%; non-attendees at 80%." / "That is 90 of 100 versus 160 of 200." / "The observed gap is ten percentage points." / "This is an association in the supplied data." / "Customers chose whether to attend." / "Baseline engagement may differ between the groups." / "Those baseline factors were not measured." / "We cannot attribute the whole difference to training." / "This does not prove that training has no effect." / "We need to know what would have happened to comparable customers without training." / "Both rates use the same renewal window." / "The result concerns these eligible customer groups." / "Adjusting measured factors would not automatically remove every unmeasured difference." / "Review a design that can better isolate the training effect." / "Use higher observed renewal among attendees." / "Keep the association and remove the unsupported promise."
Vocabulary: association: An observed relationship between variables or groups. / correlation: A measure or description of co-variation. / causal effect: A change attributable to a specified intervention. / confounding: Distortion from factors related to both exposure and outcome. / self-selection: Participation determined partly by people's own choices. / selection bias: Systematic distortion related to how observations enter groups. / baseline characteristic: A feature measured before the exposure or intervention. / motivation: A person's underlying inclination to act or engage. / engagement: Participation or involvement under a specified definition. / observational study: Analysis of naturally occurring exposures without assigned treatment. / random assignment: Allocation to groups by a defined random process. / treatment group: The group assigned or exposed to the studied intervention. / comparison group: The group used as a reference for the outcome. / counterfactual: The outcome that would occur under an alternative exposure. / renewal: Continuation of an eligible customer contract or relationship. / renewal window: The period over which renewal outcomes are observed. / eligible customer: A customer meeting the defined inclusion conditions. / absolute difference: A difference expressed in the original measure's units. / relative lift: A difference expressed relative to a comparison value. / adjustment: Accounting for specified variables in an analysis. / unmeasured factor: A relevant characteristic not recorded in the data. / generalizability: Applicability of a result beyond the studied group. / causal claim: A statement that an intervention produced an outcome. / evaluation design: The planned method for assessing a program or intervention.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-10. Stress observed association and voluntary attendance. Switch roles for turns 11-20. Keep a possible confounder distinct from a proven explanation. Check the transfer. Read both group rates without attributing the gap to training.
Partner: Priya: Data analyst / Marcus: Customer-success lead
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: In a voluntary program, 45 of 50 attendees renew and 80 of 100 non-attendees renew. Baseline engagement is unmeasured. Attendance was not randomized.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Marcus: And a mandatory program could reach people unlike the voluntary attendees. Even a stronger estimate in this group might not transfer unchanged to everyone.
Priya: That is a generalizability issue as well as a causal one. The population, intervention, and implementation matter when translating evidence into a wider operational recommendation.
Marcus: I will revise the slide to higher observed renewal among attendees, show both counts and rates, and recommend a better evaluation before claiming a guaranteed benefit.
Priya: Good. A reviewed evaluation design can address the decision we actually face. We have kept the useful finding, corrected its interpretation, and made the next evidence step clear.
END REFERENCE
05SQL, Models, and Transformation LogicThe join adds rows, not order value+

Your goal

Explain row grain and join multiplicity, diagnose a duplicated aggregate, and reject a misleading distinct-value fix.

This lesson in the book, page 40 ↗

A Read the situation

The join adds rows, not order value

Analytics engineer Lina reviews a report with analyst Owen. The complete teaching dataset has two orders: O1 is $100 and O2 is $100. Payment rows are O1: $60 and $40; O2: $100. Joining orders to payments on order ID creates three rows. Summing the repeated order amount then gives $300, although total order value is $200. SUM(DISTINCT order_amount) gives $100 because the two legitimate orders share an amount. Lina proposes aggregating payments to one row per order before joining, with key and total checks.

Lina: Analytics engineer / Owen: Reporting analyst

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. How many rows result from the supplied join?

Answer and explanation

D. Three

O1 matches two payment rows and O2 matches one, producing three joined rows.

2. What is the actual total order value?

Answer and explanation

B. $200

Two distinct orders worth one hundred dollars each total two hundred dollars.

3. Why does SUM(DISTINCT order_amount) give $100 here?

Answer and explanation

D. Both legitimate orders have the same amount, so distinct values collapse them.

Distinct amounts are not distinct orders when different orders can share the same value.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

The joined order amounts are 100, 100, and 100: O1 appears twice because it has two payments. The correct order total is 200. The payment amounts themselves are 60, 40, and 100, also totaling 200 for this dataset.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
SQL
Structured Query Language, used to query relational data.review SQL logic
row grain
What one row represents in a dataset.define the row grain
primary key
A non-null column or set of columns uniquely identifying each row.validate the primary key
foreign key
A column or set of columns constrained to reference a key in a table.check foreign-key relationships
join key
The field or fields used to match tables.choose the join key
join cardinality
The relationship in match counts between joined entities.inspect join cardinality
one-to-many relationship
One entity matching multiple related records.model a one-to-many relationship
fanout
Multiplication of rows caused by multiple join matches.detect join fanout
aggregation
Combining records into summary values.control aggregation grain
pre-aggregation
Summarizing data before another transformation or join.apply pre-aggregation
GROUP BY
SQL grouping of rows for aggregate calculations.group by order ID
SUM
An aggregate that adds non-null numeric values.check the SUM result
DISTINCT
SQL removal of duplicate values or rows in its specified scope.inspect DISTINCT semantics
COUNT
An aggregate that counts rows or non-null expressions.distinguish COUNT variants
inner join
A join returning matching combinations from both sides.inspect inner-join matches
left join
A join retaining left rows even without a right match.preserve orders with a left join
null handling
Rules for processing missing or unknown values.define null handling
filter
A condition selecting which records remain.apply the filter
CTE
Common table expression, a named query expression.use a CTE
data model
A representation of entities, relationships, and measures.review the data model
fact table
A table storing business events or measurements at a defined grain.define the fact-table grain
dimension table
A table holding descriptive attributes for analysis.maintain dimension keys
reconciliation total
A reference total used to verify transformed output.compare reconciliation totals
uniqueness test
A check that a defined key has no duplicates.run a uniqueness test

SUM(DISTINCT order_amount) removes duplicate numeric values, not duplicate order identities. It wrongly collapses the two $100 orders to one amount. Restore the intended grain and verify keys, unmatched rows, and totals instead of using a convenient-looking shortcut.

C Notice the language

Explain the row, then the total

When a report suddenly grows after a join, avoid treating the larger figure as business growth or blaming a source team without checking the transformation. A small concrete example lets technical and business colleagues inspect the same logic and agree on a verifiable repair.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Ask about grain
"What does one row represent before the join?"
Name the key
"The tables match on order ID."
Describe the relationship
"O1 has two payment rows."
Explain the fanout
"The order row is repeated for each matching payment."
Separate data from growth
"The extra row is not an extra order."
Give the wrong total
"Summing joined order amounts produces $300."
Give the reference
"The order-level total is $200."
Reject the shortcut
"Distinct amounts do not mean distinct orders."
Show the counterexample
"Both legitimate orders have an amount of $100."
Propose the repair
"Aggregate payments to one row per order before joining."
Verify the key
"Check uniqueness of order ID in the summarized payments."
Preserve unmatched orders
"The intended order population must survive the join."
Compare totals
"Reconcile the repaired output to the source order total."
Separate measures
"Order value and payment value are different measures."
Keep null rules explicit
"Define how orders without payments are represented."
Close the review
"Fix the grain and verify the result before republishing."
6 language and precision notes
Row
Not always the same as a business entity.
Many
Indicates multiple matches, not necessarily bad source data.
Distinct
Ask distinct on which fields or values.
Sum
Depends on the grain of the rows being summed.
Left join
Preserves left-side presence but does not prevent fanout.
Fixed
Requires checks beyond making one displayed total look plausible.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which operation explains the $300 result?

Answer and explanation

D. Summing order amounts after O1 is repeated for two payments

The joined order measure repeats O1's one-hundred-dollar value and inflates the aggregate.

2. Why is summing distinct amounts not a valid repair?

Answer and explanation

B. It merges different orders that happen to have equal values.

A value-level distinct operation does not deduplicate business entities by their keys.

3. Which proposed repair fits this report?

Answer and explanation

B. Summarize payments by order ID, then join at one row per order and verify totals.

Pre-aggregation by order ID preserves business identity and aligns the grain. Distinct amounts merge legitimate orders, amount joins use the wrong key, and a left join alone does not prevent multiple matches.

4. Which statement about a left join is accurate?

Answer and explanation

A. It can preserve unmatched orders but still duplicate an order with multiple matches.

Left retention and match multiplicity are separate properties of the join.

Check explanations in the book, page 93 ↗

The join adds rows, not order value

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Lina: Analytics engineer / Owen: Reporting analyst

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • join key
  • aggregation
  • one-to-many relationship
  • DISTINCT
  • fanout
  • pre-aggregation
  • uniqueness test
  • left join
  • reconciliation total
  • row grain
  1. Owen
    The report jumped from two hundred dollars of order value to three hundred after I added payment details. I thought the join would only add columns.
  2. Lina
    Check the 1. Before the join, each row represents one order. After matching O1 to two payment records, that order appears in two joined rows.
  3. Owen
    O1 is one hundred dollars with payments of sixty and forty. O2 is another one-hundred-dollar order with a single one-hundred-dollar payment.
  4. Lina
    That is a 2. The join produces three rows: two for O1 and one for O2. The third row is not evidence of another business order.
  5. Owen
    I summed the order amount after the join. Each resulting row displays one hundred, so the three displayed amounts add to three hundred.
  6. Lina
    That is join 3. The payment rows are legitimate, but the order-level measure is repeated. We need to preserve its meaning instead of treating each joined occurrence as new value.
  7. Owen
    SUM DISTINCT brought it down to one hundred. That looks worse: we have two separate orders worth one hundred each. Have I removed the wrong duplicate?
  8. Lina
    Exactly. 4 applies to the selected value here. Two different orders can both be worth one hundred dollars, so amount equality does not establish duplicate business identity.
  9. Owen
    The correct order total is two hundred. The payment amounts also total two hundred in this small dataset, but that does not make the measures identical.
  10. Lina
    Keep the 5 tied to the intended entity. An order-value report and a payment-value report answer different questions, even when their totals happen to match in this example.
  11. Owen
    For the order report, I can summarize the payment side first so there is one payment-summary row for each order ID.
  12. Lina
    Yes, use 6. Then verify that the payment summary has only one row per order. Joining two aligned grains avoids repeating the original order amount for every payment.
  13. Owen
    The grouping field should be order ID, not amount. Grouping by amount would combine the two orders again because they both have the same value.
  14. Lina
    Correct. The 7 must preserve the intended relationship. Test the summary key and check that the matching logic uses the appropriate identifier on both sides.
  15. Owen
    This teaching dataset has payments for both orders. In the full report, an order without a payment should still appear if it belongs to the reporting population.
  16. Lina
    Then choose and test the appropriate 8. It can preserve unmatched orders, but it would not by itself solve multiple matches. Population preservation and multiplicity are separate checks.
  17. Owen
    I will check both the two-order count and the two-hundred-dollar total. Could those totals look right even if one missing order offsets a duplicate?
  18. Lina
    Use the original 9 as a reference and inspect differences. A plausible-looking grand total alone is not enough if missing orders and duplicated orders happen to offset each other.
  19. Owen
    My explanation will say the join repeated an order-level amount; it did not create revenue. I will withhold the inflated result while the transformation is corrected.
  20. Lina
    Add a 10 for the summarized order key and document the grain. That makes the repair repeatable rather than relying on someone noticing another surprising total later.

Answers and explanations
  1. row grain Turn 2. Row grain describes what each row represents, which changes when an order is matched to multiple payment rows.
  2. one-to-many relationship Turn 4. The one-to-many relationship lets one order match multiple payment records, producing more than one output row for O1.
  3. fanout Turn 6. Fanout multiplies the order row through payment matches, so summing the repeated order measure inflates its total.
  4. DISTINCT Turn 8. DISTINCT on the amount removes equal numeric values, not repeated order identities, so both legitimate hundred-dollar orders collapse.
  5. aggregation Turn 10. Aggregation combines rows at a chosen grain; the measure and that grain must match to avoid repeated order values.
  6. pre-aggregation Turn 12. Pre-aggregation summarizes payments before the join so each order matches at most one summarized payment row.
  7. join key Turn 14. The join key is order ID, which identifies the business relationship rather than a coincidentally equal amount.
  8. left join Turn 16. A left join can retain orders without a matching payment summary, but its retention behavior does not itself prevent fanout.
  9. reconciliation total Turn 18. The reconciliation total is the two-hundred-dollar source order value used to verify that the transformed report preserves the intended measure.
  10. uniqueness test Turn 20. The uniqueness test checks one row per order ID in the summarized data so the corrected relationship can be maintained.

Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Several payment rows multiply order revenue

Fictional SQL review using the lesson case: order rows join to several payment rows per order, and reported revenue increases after the join.

  1. Reporting analyst: Revenue grows after I join payments. Could you clarify the grain of each source table?
  2. Data engineer: Orders has one row per order; payments can have several rows for the same order.
  3. Reporting analyst: Then an order's revenue may appear repeatedly when its row matches multiple payment records.
  4. Data engineer: Yes. Check that relationship before assuming the larger total represents additional business activity.
  5. Reporting analyst: Would adding DISTINCT to the SQL automatically fix the report without changing its meaning?
  6. Data engineer: Not necessarily. We need the intended reporting grain and measure definition before choosing the transformation.
  7. Reporting analyst: The report is intended to summarize order revenue. I'll confirm how payment details should contribute.
  8. Data engineer: Please inspect matched row counts and a small traceable example with the same join logic.
  9. Reporting analyst: I'll retain the original totals and compare the transformation against those known source records.
  10. Data engineer: Then we can choose logic that preserves the intended unit rather than hide multiplication with an unexplained filter.
A customer changes regions over time

Fictional data-model review: the customer table stores multiple dated region records, and a report joins orders using customer ID alone.

  1. BI developer: My regional order report duplicates some orders. Does the customer table contain one row per customer?
  2. Data modeler: No. It contains dated region records, so a customer can have several rows over time.
  3. BI developer: Then joining only on customer ID doesn't specify which historical region should apply to an order.
  4. Data modeler: Correct. We need the reporting question: region at order time or the customer's current region?
  5. BI developer: That business rule isn't documented. I shouldn't choose the latest row simply because it removes duplicates.
  6. Data modeler: Exactly. A convenient SQL result could answer the wrong business question even with plausible totals.
  7. BI developer: I'll ask the metric owner which time relationship is intended before changing the join logic.
  8. Data modeler: Please also check how overlapping or missing validity periods should be handled, rather than silently guessing.
  9. BI developer: I'll bring those edge cases with a small sample and keep the current report marked under review.
  10. Data modeler: Then the transformation can reflect an explicit grain and time rule instead of accidental matching.
A daily snapshot is not a daily addition

Fictional warehouse report: an inventory table contains daily balance snapshots, and a draft report sums them as if they were daily receipts.

  1. Operations analyst: The monthly inventory number looks huge. Does this table record daily receipts or daily balance snapshots?
  2. Data engineer: It records a balance snapshot for each item each day, not newly received units.
  3. Operations analyst: Then summing every day's balance counts the same continuing stock repeatedly across the month.
  4. Data engineer: Correct. We need to clarify whether the report wants an ending balance, an average, or another measure.
  5. Operations analyst: I shouldn't select month-end automatically because the business question hasn't been confirmed yet.
  6. Data engineer: Exactly. First establish the measure and grain; then choose the SQL transformation that answers it.
  7. Operations analyst: Could you identify the snapshot date field and any days missing from the source?
  8. Data engineer: I'll provide that context, but the business owner still needs to confirm the intended inventory question.
  9. Operations analyst: I'll ask them and compare a small item history before changing the dashboard calculation.
  10. Data engineer: Good. The corrected logic should reflect what the rows mean, not merely produce a smaller number.

Lesson 05: SQL, Models, and Transformation Logic
Original fictional case: Analytics engineer Lina reviews a report with analyst Owen. The complete teaching dataset has two orders: O1 is $100 and O2 is $100. Payment rows are O1: $60 and $40; O2: $100. Joining orders to payments on order ID creates three rows. Summing the repeated order amount then gives $300, although total order value is $200. Language workshop: The join adds rows, not order value
Communication goal: Explain row grain and join multiplicity, diagnose a duplicated aggregate, and reject a misleading distinct-value fix.
Language guidance: The joined order amounts are 100, 100, and 100: O1 appears twice because it has two payments. The correct order total is 200. The payment amounts themselves are 60, 40, and 100, also totaling 200 for this dataset.

Vocabulary: SQL: Structured Query Language, used to query relational data. / row grain: What one row represents in a dataset. / primary key: A non-null column or set of columns uniquely identifying each row. / foreign key: A column or set of columns constrained to reference a key in a table. / join key: The field or fields used to match tables. / join cardinality: The relationship in match counts between joined entities. / one-to-many relationship: One entity matching multiple related records. / fanout: Multiplication of rows caused by multiple join matches. / aggregation: Combining records into summary values. / pre-aggregation: Summarizing data before another transformation or join. / GROUP BY: SQL grouping of rows for aggregate calculations. / SUM: An aggregate that adds non-null numeric values. / DISTINCT: SQL removal of duplicate values or rows in its specified scope. / COUNT: An aggregate that counts rows or non-null expressions. / inner join: A join returning matching combinations from both sides. / left join: A join retaining left rows even without a right match. / null handling: Rules for processing missing or unknown values. / filter: A condition selecting which records remain. / CTE: Common table expression, a named query expression. / data model: A representation of entities, relationships, and measures. / fact table: A table storing business events or measurements at a defined grain. / dimension table: A table holding descriptive attributes for analysis. / reconciliation total: A reference total used to verify transformed output. / uniqueness test: A check that a defined key has no duplicates.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-10. Contrast an order identity with its amount. Switch roles for turns 11-20. Stress one row per order and both reconciliation checks. Check the transfer. Read the correct total, inflated sum, and distinct-value sum separately.
Partner: Lina: Analytics engineer / Owen: Reporting analyst

Optional second-round challenge: Order A is $80 with two $40 payments. Order B is $80 with one $80 payment. An order-to-payment join yields three rows. The report must count each order amount once.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Owen: I will check both the two-order count and the two-hundred-dollar total. Could those totals look right even if one missing order offsets a duplicate?
Lina: Use the original reconciliation total as a reference and inspect differences. A plausible-looking grand total alone is not enough if missing orders and duplicated orders happen to offset each other.
Owen: My explanation will say the join repeated an order-level amount; it did not create revenue. I will withhold the inflated result while the transformation is corrected.
Lina: Add a uniqueness test for the summarized order key and document the grain. That makes the repair repeatable rather than relying on someone noticing another surprising total later.
END REFERENCE
