Lesson 05: SQL, Models, and Transformation Logic
Original fictional case: Analytics engineer Lina reviews a report with analyst Owen. The complete teaching dataset has two orders: O1 is $100 and O2 is $100. Payment rows are O1: $60 and $40; O2: $100. Joining orders to payments on order ID creates three rows. Summing the repeated order amount then gives $300, although total order value is $200. SUM(DISTINCT order_amount) gives $100 because the two legitimate orders share an amount. Lina proposes aggregating payments to one row per order before joining, with key and total checks.
Language workshop: The join adds rows, not order value
Communication goal: Explain row grain and join multiplicity, diagnose a duplicated aggregate, and reject a misleading distinct-value fix.
Language guidance: The joined order amounts are 100, 100, and 100: O1 appears twice because it has two payments. The correct order total is 200. The payment amounts themselves are 60, 40, and 100, also totaling 200 for this dataset.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): "What does one row represent before the join?" / "The tables match on order ID." / "O1 has two payment rows." / "The order row is repeated for each matching payment." / "The extra row is not an extra order." / "Summing joined order amounts produces $300." / "The order-level total is $200." / "Distinct amounts do not mean distinct orders." / "Both legitimate orders have an amount of $100." / "Aggregate payments to one row per order before joining." / "Check uniqueness of order ID in the summarized payments." / "The intended order population must survive the join." / "Reconcile the repaired output to the source order total." / "Order value and payment value are different measures." / "Define how orders without payments are represented." / "Fix the grain and verify the result before republishing."
Vocabulary: SQL: Structured Query Language, used to query relational data. / row grain: What one row represents in a dataset. / primary key: A non-null column or set of columns uniquely identifying each row. / foreign key: A column or set of columns constrained to reference a key in a table. / join key: The field or fields used to match tables. / join cardinality: The relationship in match counts between joined entities. / one-to-many relationship: One entity matching multiple related records. / fanout: Multiplication of rows caused by multiple join matches. / aggregation: Combining records into summary values. / pre-aggregation: Summarizing data before another transformation or join. / GROUP BY: SQL grouping of rows for aggregate calculations. / SUM: An aggregate that adds non-null numeric values. / DISTINCT: SQL removal of duplicate values or rows in its specified scope. / COUNT: An aggregate that counts rows or non-null expressions. / inner join: A join returning matching combinations from both sides. / left join: A join retaining left rows even without a right match. / null handling: Rules for processing missing or unknown values. / filter: A condition selecting which records remain. / CTE: Common table expression, a named query expression. / data model: A representation of entities, relationships, and measures. / fact table: A table storing business events or measurements at a defined grain. / dimension table: A table holding descriptive attributes for analysis. / reconciliation total: A reference total used to verify transformed output. / uniqueness test: A check that a defined key has no duplicates.

Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.

06Experiment Readouts and Statistical ThinkingA positive estimate is not the rollout rule+

Your goal

Present an experiment estimate with its interval, practical threshold, and pre-agreed decision rule.

This lesson in the book, page 49 ↗

A Read the situation

A positive estimate is not the rollout rule

Experiment analyst Theo reviews a completed randomized test with product director Hana. The validated readout estimates a 1.2-percentage-point increase in completion, with a 95% confidence interval from -0.4 to +2.8 points. The pre-agreed rollout rule requires the interval lower bound to exceed +1.0 point, with guardrails passing. Guardrails passed, but the lower-bound condition did not. The slide currently shows only +1.2. The test has reached its planned endpoint; no unplanned extension or new rollout approval is authorized.

Theo: Experiment analyst / Hana: Product director

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What is the point estimate?

Answer and explanation

C. +1.2 percentage points

The supplied central effect estimate is positive one point two percentage points.

2. Did the lower-bound rule pass?

Answer and explanation

A. No; -0.4 does not exceed +1.0.

The rule concerns the interval lower bound, not the positive point estimate.

3. What stage has the test reached?

Answer and explanation

B. Its planned endpoint

The briefing explicitly says the planned endpoint has been reached without new authorization.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

The point estimate is +1.2 points, but the interval runs from -0.4 to +2.8. It includes zero and values below the practical threshold. Under the supplied rule, a passing guardrail cannot compensate for a failing lower bound.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
randomized experiment
A study allocating exposure through a random process.interpret a randomized experiment
point estimate
A single value estimating an unknown quantity.report the point estimate
interval estimate
A range estimating an unknown quantity under a method.report an interval estimate
confidence interval
An interval from a method with specified long-run coverage.interpret a confidence interval
confidence level
The nominal long-run coverage rate of the interval method.state the confidence level
lower bound
The smaller endpoint of an interval.compare the lower bound
upper bound
The larger endpoint of an interval.report the upper bound
effect size
The magnitude of a difference or relationship.assess effect size
no-change value
The parameter value representing no effect on the stated scale.identify the no-change value
practical threshold
The effect boundary relevant to a business decision.set a practical threshold
decision rule
A predefined condition governing a conclusion or action.apply the decision rule
guardrail
A measure limiting unacceptable side effects.check the guardrails
primary outcome
The main measure used to assess the intervention.specify the primary outcome
planned endpoint
The pre-agreed point for completing the analysis or test.respect the planned endpoint
statistical significance
A result meeting a specified statistical testing criterion.qualify statistical significance
practical significance
Importance of an effect for a real decision.assess practical significance
null hypothesis
A specified claim assessed by a statistical test.state the null hypothesis
standard error
The standard deviation of an estimator's sampling distribution, usually estimated.report the standard error
sampling variability
Variation in estimates across possible samples.account for sampling variability
statistical power
Probability of detecting a specified effect under assumptions.plan statistical power
minimum detectable effect
An effect size used in the study's power planning.specify the minimum detectable effect
sequential analysis
A method allowing planned repeated statistical decisions.use a valid sequential analysis
equivalence
Similarity within a predefined acceptable difference margin.test equivalence appropriately
inconclusive result
Evidence insufficient for the specified conclusion.explain an inconclusive result

A 95% confidence method has a stated long-run coverage property under its assumptions; it does not make the realized fixed parameter 95% likely to lie in this particular interval. An interval including zero also does not prove equivalence or no effect.

C Notice the language

Distinguish a promising signal from a passed rule

A positive estimate can be worth discussing without being a win under the actual decision standard. Show the interval and threshold together. Correcting an overconfident slide should preserve the evidence, not replace optimism with an equally unsupported claim of no effect.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Report the center
"The estimated effect is plus 1.2 percentage points."
Show the uncertainty
"The 95% interval runs from minus 0.4 to plus 2.8 points."
Name the no-change value
"The interval includes zero."
Keep the scale explicit
"These are percentage points, not relative percentages."
State the actual rule
"The lower bound must exceed plus 1.0 point."
Apply the rule
"The lower-bound condition was not met."
Preserve the guardrail result
"The guardrails passed."
Separate the conditions
"Passing guardrails does not replace the effect requirement."
Avoid declaring a winner
"This readout does not authorize rollout under our rule."
Avoid declaring no effect
"It also does not prove the true effect is zero."
Respect the endpoint
"The planned test has ended."
Reject opportunistic extension
"We cannot keep collecting until the result looks favorable."
Discuss next work honestly
"Any new study needs an explicitly reviewed design."
Keep the interval visible
"Place both bounds beside the estimate."
Distinguish decision from interest
"The signal may merit follow-up without passing this launch rule."
Close the readout
"Report the estimate, interval, guardrails, and unmet decision condition together."
6 language and precision notes
Positive
Describes the estimate's sign, not guaranteed benefit.
Includes zero
Does not establish an exact zero effect.
95%
Describes the method's confidence level, not every possible uncertainty.
Significant
Distinguish statistical criteria from business importance.
Failed rule
Not the same as proof that an intervention is useless.
More data
Needs a justified design, not a search for a preferred result.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which slide is most faithful to the readout?

Answer and explanation

D. +1.2 points; 95% interval -0.4 to +2.8; guardrails pass; rollout criterion unmet

The correct slide retains percentage-point units, the negative lower bound, and both conditions of the rollout rule. The alternatives change the decision, scale, or sign.

2. Why does the +1.2 estimate not satisfy the rule?

Answer and explanation

C. The rule uses the interval lower bound, which is -0.4.

The pre-agreed rule explicitly concerns the lower endpoint exceeding one point.

3. What would be an unsupported next step?

Answer and explanation

D. Extend the finished test repeatedly until the original method produces a favorable result.

Opportunistic extension changes the analysis behavior without the approved method needed for valid inference.

4. Which statement about zero is correct?

Answer and explanation

D. Its inclusion does not prove either exact zero effect or equivalence.

An interval containing zero permits uncertainty and does not establish an equivalence conclusion.

Check explanations in the book, page 95 ↗

E Conversations

A positive estimate is not the rollout rule

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Theo: Experiment analyst / Hana: Product director

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank

  • lower bound
  • practical significance
  • planned endpoint
  • decision rule
  • practical threshold
  • point estimate

  • sequential analysis
  1. Hana
    The slide says plus one point two, which looks promising. Can we call the test a win and move to rollout now that it has finished?
  2. Theo
    Show the 1 with the interval. The estimated increase is one point two percentage points, but the ninety-five-percent interval extends from minus zero point four to plus two point eight.
  3. Hana
    That range includes a small negative effect and zero as well as useful positive outcomes. The current slide hides those possibilities by showing only the center.
  4. Theo
    Correct. The 2 belongs beside the estimate. It prevents us from presenting a promising central value as if the uncertainty were negligible or entirely favorable.
  5. Hana
    Remind me of the actual rollout rule. I remember that we set a one-percentage-point threshold and also required the guardrails to pass.
  6. Theo
    The 3 must exceed plus one point zero, with guardrails passing. Our lower endpoint is minus zero point four, so that condition was not met.
  7. Hana
    The guardrails did pass, which is good news. But that is a separate condition, not a substitute for the evidence requirement on completion improvement.
  8. Theo
    Exactly. Apply the whole 4. We can acknowledge the guardrail result while saying the test did not authorize rollout under the standard agreed before seeing these results.
  9. Hana
    Then should the slide say no effect? Zero is inside the interval, but so are positive effects. I do not want to replace one overclaim with another.
  10. Theo
    No. The 5 is included, but useful positive effects are included too. The readout does not establish exact zero or show that the two experiences are equivalent.
  11. Hana
    Then the decision is clearer than the underlying effect. We know the supplied rollout rule failed, while the magnitude and even direction remain uncertain.
  12. Theo
    That is an 6 for the desired benefit claim, not permission to hide the estimate. We should report what the evidence supports and what decision it does not support.
  13. Hana
    Could we extend it until the lower bound clears one point? Or would that change the rule after we have already seen the result?
  14. Theo
    We reached the 7. Continuing until the result looks favorable changes the statistical behavior. There is no authorized extension or approved repeated-decision method in the supplied plan.
  15. Hana
    A new study might still be justified, but it would need a clear design and rationale rather than an attempt to rescue the original headline.
  16. Theo
    Yes. An appropriate 8 can support planned repeated decisions, but that is not what was specified here. Any new evaluation needs its own reviewed assumptions and rules.
  17. Hana
    I will also keep the units visible. Plus one point two means percentage points, not a one-point-two-percent relative increase from an unstated baseline.
  18. Theo
    Good. The 9 should be displayed on the same scale. Statistical uncertainty and business value are related in the decision, but they are not interchangeable labels.
  19. Hana
    My revised readout will show the center, both bounds, passing guardrails, and the unmet rollout condition. No launch approval or no-effect conclusion will be implied.
  20. Theo
    That preserves 10 as a separate question. The signal may merit well-designed follow-up, but this completed test has not passed the agreed rule for rollout.

Answers and explanations
  1. point estimate Turn 2. The point estimate is the single plus-1.2-point estimate, which must be interpreted with its uncertainty and the decision rule.
  2. confidence interval Turn 4. The confidence interval communicates uncertainty under its statistical method instead of treating the estimate as a guaranteed effect.
  3. lower bound Turn 6. The lower bound is minus 0.4 points, which fails the requirement that it exceed plus 1.0 point.
  4. decision rule Turn 8. The decision rule requires both the lower-bound condition and passing guardrails, so one passing condition cannot replace the other.
  5. no-change value Turn 10. Zero is the no-change value on the difference scale, but its presence in the interval does not prove the effect is exactly zero.
  6. inconclusive result Turn 12. The result is inconclusive for the desired positive-effect conclusion, while the supplied rollout rule has a definite unmet condition.
  7. planned endpoint Turn 14. The planned endpoint has already been reached, so an opportunistic extension would change the agreed analysis behavior.
  8. sequential analysis Turn 16. Sequential analysis is designed for planned repeated decisions; it cannot be assumed to justify an unapproved extension of this completed test.
  9. practical threshold Turn 18. The practical threshold is the one-point business-relevant boundary used in the agreed lower-bound decision condition.
  10. practical significance Turn 20. Practical significance concerns whether an effect matters for the business, which is not established merely by a positive central estimate.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Hana: The slide says plus one point two, which looks promising. Can we call the test a win and move to rollout now that it has finished?
  2. Theo: Show the point estimate with the interval. The estimated increase is one point two percentage points, but the ninety-five-percent interval extends from minus zero point four to plus two point eight.

  4. Theo: Correct. The confidence interval belongs beside the estimate. It prevents us from presenting a promising central value as if the uncertainty were negligible or entirely favorable.

  6. Theo: The lower bound must exceed plus one point zero, with guardrails passing. Our lower endpoint is minus zero point four, so that condition was not met.

Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

The estimate needs its uncertainty interval

Fictional experiment readout using the lesson case: the point estimate is positive, but its interval includes values near no change; the slide omits the interval.

  1. Decision sponsor: The slide shows a positive effect. Can I treat that number as the established improvement?
  2. Experiment analyst: It is the point estimate. Its confidence interval includes values close to no change, which the slide omits.
  3. Decision sponsor: So the estimate summarizes the observed result, but the uncertainty affects how strongly we should interpret it.
  4. Experiment analyst: Yes. We should present the effect size and interval together, using the method's stated assumptions.
  5. Decision sponsor: Does an interval near no change prove that the treatment has no useful effect?
  6. Experiment analyst: No. It indicates uncertainty under the analysis; it doesn't justify that opposite certainty either.
  7. Decision sponsor: Please also include sample size and the guardrail results so one favorable number doesn't carry the decision.
  8. Experiment analyst: I'll check those records and distinguish completed analysis from any results still pending.
  9. Decision sponsor: Then the recommendation can reflect practical importance and uncertainty instead of promising the point estimate.
  10. Experiment analyst: Agreed. I'll revise the readout so its conclusion stays within the evidence we actually have.
A relative lift needs the absolute change

Fictional experiment: conversion rises from 2% to 2.2%; uncertainty analysis and guardrail review are not complete.

  1. Product sponsor: A ten percent lift sounds substantial. Is that the clearest way to present this conversion result?
  2. Data scientist: Include the absolute change too: two percent to two-point-two percent is a zero-point-two percentage-point increase.
  3. Product sponsor: Then ten percent describes relative change, not ten additional percentage points of conversion.
  4. Data scientist: Correct. Both can be reported, but the distinction matters when judging practical effect size.
  5. Product sponsor: Can the size of the relative lift tell us the result is statistically reliable?
  6. Data scientist: Not by itself. Uncertainty analysis is incomplete, and the guardrail review is still pending.
  7. Product sponsor: I'll keep the observed rates but avoid calling the treatment a confirmed success today.
  8. Data scientist: Please also include the sample-size context once verified, rather than presenting the percentage without its basis.
  9. Product sponsor: At the readout, we'll ask whether the effect is useful and supported, not just whether it sounds large.
  10. Data scientist: I'll bring the completed analysis and clearly identify any remaining uncertainty before that decision.
An unplanned subgroup result needs a caveat

Fictional experiment review: the overall estimate is modest, and an exploratory subgroup shows a larger estimate; the subgroup was selected after examining results.

  1. Business lead: This subgroup has a much larger estimate. Can we make it the headline experiment result?
  2. Experiment analyst: It was selected after we examined the results, so we should label that analysis exploratory.
  3. Business lead: Does exploratory mean the observed subgroup estimate is wrong and should be removed entirely?
  4. Experiment analyst: No. It can suggest a follow-up question, but it doesn't have the same status as the planned comparison.
  5. Business lead: We also need its sample size and uncertainty, rather than assume a bigger estimate is more dependable.
  6. Experiment analyst: Exactly. Smaller subgroups and multiple examined comparisons can complicate interpretation under the chosen analysis.
  7. Business lead: I'll keep the overall result visible and describe the subgroup as a hypothesis-generating observation.
  8. Experiment analyst: Good. We should retain the selection history instead of rewriting it as a prespecified test.
  9. Business lead: Could we propose a targeted future evaluation without promising the larger effect will repeat?
  10. Experiment analyst: Yes. That is a bounded recommendation, with the current evidence and guardrail questions clearly stated.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

A positive estimate is not the rollout rule
  1. Read turns 1-10. Say minus and plus clearly for both interval endpoints.
  2. Switch roles for turns 11-20. Contrast an unmet rollout rule with proof of no effect.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Read the endpoint named in the rule

An experiment estimates +0.8 points with a 95% interval from -0.2 to +1.8. The rule requires the lower bound to exceed +0.5, and guardrails pass. The planned test has ended.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Analyst: "The point estimate is plus ___ points."

Answer and explanation

D. 0.8

The supplied central estimate is positive zero point eight percentage points.

2. Director: "The lower bound is minus ___ points."

Answer and explanation

C. 0.2

The interval begins at negative zero point two percentage points.

3. Analyst: "The required boundary is plus ___ points."

Answer and explanation

B. 0.5

The stated lower-bound rule uses positive zero point five as its threshold.

4. Director: "The rollout condition is ___."

Answer and explanation

C. unmet

The negative lower bound does not exceed the required positive threshold.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Lesson 06: Experiment Readouts and Statistical Thinking
Original fictional case: Experiment analyst Theo reviews a completed randomized test with product director Hana. The validated readout estimates a 1.2-percentage-point increase in completion, with a 95% confidence interval from -0.4 to +2.8 points. The pre-agreed rollout rule requires the interval lower bound to exceed +1.0 point, with guardrails passing. Guardrails passed, but the lower-bound condition did not. The slide currently shows only +1.2. The test has reached its planned endpoint; no unplanned extension or new rollout approval is authorized.
Language workshop: A positive estimate is not the rollout rule
Communication goal: Present an experiment estimate with its interval, practical threshold, and pre-agreed decision rule.
Language guidance: The point estimate is +1.2 points, but the interval runs from -0.4 to +2.8. It includes zero and values below the practical threshold. Under the supplied rule, a passing guardrail cannot compensate for a failing lower bound.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): "The estimated effect is plus 1.2 percentage points." / "The 95% interval runs from minus 0.4 to plus 2.8 points." / "The interval includes zero." / "These are percentage points, not relative percentages." / "The lower bound must exceed plus 1.0 point." / "The lower-bound condition was not met." / "The guardrails passed." / "Passing guardrails does not replace the effect requirement." / "This readout does not authorize rollout under our rule." / "It also does not prove the true effect is zero." / "The planned test has ended." / "We cannot keep collecting until the result looks favorable." / "Any new study needs an explicitly reviewed design." / "Place both bounds beside the estimate." / "The signal may merit follow-up without passing this launch rule." / "Report the estimate, interval, guardrails, and unmet decision condition together."
Vocabulary: randomized experiment: A study allocating exposure through a random process. / point estimate: A single value estimating an unknown quantity. / interval estimate: A range estimating an unknown quantity under a method. / confidence interval: An interval from a method with specified long-run coverage. / confidence level: The nominal long-run coverage rate of the interval method. / lower bound: The smaller endpoint of an interval. / upper bound: The larger endpoint of an interval. / effect size: The magnitude of a difference or relationship. / no-change value: The parameter value representing no effect on the stated scale. / practical threshold: The effect boundary relevant to a business decision. / decision rule: A predefined condition governing a conclusion or action. / guardrail: A measure limiting unacceptable side effects. / primary outcome: The main measure used to assess the intervention. / planned endpoint: The pre-agreed point for completing the analysis or test. / statistical significance: A result meeting a specified statistical testing criterion. / practical significance: Importance of an effect for a real decision. / null hypothesis: A specified claim assessed by a statistical test. / standard error: The standard deviation of an estimator's sampling distribution, usually estimated. / sampling variability: Variation in estimates across possible samples. / statistical power: Probability of detecting a specified effect under assumptions. / minimum detectable effect: An effect size used in the study's power planning. / sequential analysis: A method allowing planned repeated statistical decisions. / equivalence: Similarity within a predefined acceptable difference margin. / inconclusive result: Evidence insufficient for the specified conclusion.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-10. Say minus and plus clearly for both interval endpoints. Switch roles for turns 11-20. Contrast an unmet rollout rule with proof of no effect. Check the transfer. Stress the lower bound rather than the positive point estimate.
Partner: Theo: Experiment analyst / Hana: Product director
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: An experiment estimates +0.8 points with a 95% interval from -0.2 to +1.8. The rule requires the lower bound to exceed +0.5, and guardrails pass. The planned test has ended.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Hana: I will also keep the units visible. Plus one point two means percentage points, not a one-point-two-percent relative increase from an unstated baseline.
Theo: Good. The practical threshold should be displayed on the same scale. Statistical uncertainty and business value are related in the decision, but they are not interchangeable labels.
Hana: My revised readout will show the center, both bounds, passing guardrails, and the unmet rollout condition. No launch approval or no-effect conclusion will be implied.
Theo: That preserves practical significance as a separate question. The signal may merit well-designed follow-up, but this completed test has not passed the agreed rule for rollout.
END REFERENCE

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

07Data Governance and PrivacyThe analysis needs weekly counts, not customer names+

Your goal

Specify a purpose-limited data request, distinguish pseudonymization from anonymity, and preserve approval and retention controls.

This lesson in the book, page 58 ↗

A Read the situation

The analysis needs weekly counts, not customer names

Analyst Sara asks data steward Malik for a full customer export to compare weekly late-resolution rates by product tier. The supplied analysis can use week, tier, eligible-case count, and late-case count; names, emails, phone numbers, and free-text comments are unnecessary. A team proposal uses a reviewed aggregate table in the approved workspace with 30-day access, subject to owner approval and disclosure review. Approval is pending. Small groups still require review. Replacing names with stable IDs would not automatically make row-level data anonymous or remove controls.

Sara: Data analyst / Malik: Data steward

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. Which fields directly support the supplied analysis?

Answer and explanation

B. Week, tier, eligible-case count, and late-case count

The question concerns weekly tier-level rates, which the four specified aggregate fields can support.

2. What is the current approval status?

Answer and explanation

A. Pending

The briefing explicitly leaves owner approval and review incomplete.

3. What does replacing names with stable IDs establish?

Answer and explanation

B. It does not automatically establish anonymity.

Stable identifiers may still allow linkage, so name removal alone does not establish anonymity.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

The rate needs late-case count divided by eligible-case count within each week and tier. Names and contact details do not support that supplied calculation. Data minimization means enough relevant data for the purpose, not an unusably stripped dataset.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
data governance
Responsibilities and rules for managing data use.establish data governance
data steward
A role supporting defined data quality and handling responsibilities.consult the data steward
data owner
The role accountable for specified data decisions.obtain data-owner approval
purpose limitation
Restricting data use to defined appropriate purposes.document the analysis purpose
data minimization
Limiting data to what the stated purpose needs.apply data minimization
personal data
Information relating to an identifiable person under the applicable rules.assess personal-data handling
direct identifier
A field that directly identifies a person.remove unnecessary direct identifiers
indirect identifier
A field that may identify someone when combined with other information.review indirect identifiers
pseudonymization
Replacing identifying details while retaining possible linkage.assess pseudonymization safeguards
anonymization
Processing intended to prevent identification under the relevant standard.evaluate anonymization
re-identification risk
The possibility of connecting data back to an individual.assess re-identification risk
aggregation
Combining records into group-level summaries.review aggregation level
small cell
A reported group with few contributing observations.review small cells
disclosure review
Assessment of risks from releasing or sharing information.conduct disclosure review
access scope
The specific data and actions a permission covers.limit access scope
retention period
The duration for which data is kept under defined rules.document the retention period
access expiry
The point when an access permission ends.enforce access expiry
approved workspace
An environment authorized for the stated data work.use the approved workspace
data lineage
The traceable path of data through sources and transformations.document data lineage
data catalog
An organized inventory of datasets and their metadata.consult the data catalog
processing purpose
The defined reason for collecting or using data.state the processing purpose
permitted audience
The people authorized to receive a result or dataset.define the permitted audience
export restriction
A limit on moving data outside an approved environment.enforce export restrictions
secondary use
A use beyond the original stated purpose.review proposed secondary use

Thirty-day access is the proposed local permission duration, not a universal legal retention rule. Access expiry and data deletion are separate matters. Aggregation or stable-ID substitution does not automatically remove disclosure risks or approval requirements.

C Notice the language

Tie each field to the question

A constructive data-access discussion explains what information the analysis requires and offers a usable alternative. Avoid assuming that analysts need everything or that every aggregate is harmless. Keep the purpose, approval, audience, and handling conditions visible in the same request.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Define the purpose
"We are comparing weekly late-resolution rates by tier."
Link fields to the task
"The calculation needs counts by week and tier."
Limit identifiers
"Names and contact details are unnecessary for this question."
Offer an alternative
"Use the reviewed aggregate table."
Preserve the denominator
"Include eligible-case counts as well as late-case counts."
Keep approval visible
"Owner approval is still pending."
State the environment
"The work belongs in the approved workspace."
Bound access
"The proposed access duration is thirty days."
Separate duration rules
"Access expiry is not automatically the data-retention rule."
Check group disclosure
"Small groups still need disclosure review."
Avoid an anonymity claim
"Stable IDs do not automatically make the records anonymous."
Control the audience
"Confirm who may receive the output."
Keep exports governed
"Do not assume approval permits an external export."
Address new purposes
"A different question may need a new review."
Record the source
"Keep the source and transformation lineage."
Close the request
"Submit the purpose, four fields, workspace, duration, and review conditions."
6 language and precision notes
Necessary
Explain the connection between each field and the question.
Aggregate
A format, not an automatic privacy determination.
Anonymous
A conclusion requiring the applicable assessment.
Pseudonymous
May still allow linkage and require protection.
Thirty days
A case-specific access proposal, not a legal rule.
Approved
State exactly which purpose, audience, and actions are covered.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which request best matches the supplied need?

Answer and explanation

A. Reviewed weekly tier-level counts in the approved workspace, with pending approvals and 30-day access

The correct request supplies the needed measures while preserving the stated handling conditions.

2. Why include eligible-case count?

Answer and explanation

A. It provides the denominator for each rate.

A late-case count alone cannot show the proportion of eligible cases affected.

3. Which statement about aggregation is accurate?

Answer and explanation

A. It can reduce detail but still needs the stated disclosure review.

The case explicitly requires review, including small-group risks, despite the aggregate format.

4. What does 30-day access specify?

Answer and explanation

C. The proposed duration of permission in this case

The briefing defines a local access proposal, not a general retention law or completed approval.

Check explanations in the book, page 97 ↗

E Conversations

The analysis needs weekly counts, not customer names

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Sara: Data analyst / Malik: Data steward

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • Access expiry
  • disclosure review
  • permitted audience
  • secondary use
  • pseudonymization
  • data minimization
  • data lineage
  • processing purpose
  • aggregation
  • access scope
  1. Sara
    I requested the full customer export for weekly late-resolution rates by product tier. I thought extra fields would give us flexibility.
  2. Malik
    Start with the 1. The supplied question needs week, tier, eligible-case count, and late-case count. Which part requires names, contact details, or unrestricted free-text comments?
  3. Sara
    Those extra fields do not enter the rate. I need late cases divided by eligible cases within each week and tier.
  4. Malik
    Then 2 supports a narrower request. We should retain what makes the analysis valid, including its denominator, without adding unrelated personal fields simply because they might be interesting later.
  5. Sara
    An aggregate table would work for this comparison. I do not need individual rows if the counts use the agreed definitions and can be traced to their source.
  6. Malik
    That 3 is a useful alternative, subject to review. We still need to check the output, especially where a group is small enough to raise identification or disclosure concerns.
  7. Sara
    So a small tier in one week should not automatically be published just because its row contains counts rather than names. The audience and context still matter.
  8. Malik
    Correct. A 4 remains part of the proposal. Do not treat a change in table shape as an automatic conclusion that all privacy and handling conditions have disappeared.
  9. Sara
    If someone later asks for individual records, could replacing customer names with stable IDs make that request anonymous and remove the approval requirement?
  10. Malik
    That is usually a 5 question, not an automatic anonymity claim. Stable identifiers can still support linkage, and the applicable assessment and access controls must remain explicit.
  11. Sara
    For this task, I will stay with the reviewed aggregate fields. The proposal also specifies the approved workspace rather than an unrestricted download to any location.
  12. Malik
    Keep that in the 6. Owner approval is still pending. A technically convenient format does not authorize an export or make the request approved before the decision is recorded.
  13. Sara
    Does thirty-day access also mean deleting the data on day thirty? I need to distinguish the permission expiry from the actual retention rule.
  14. Malik
    Exactly. 7 and data retention are separate questions. The actual retention and deletion arrangements need their own approved basis rather than being inferred from this access duration.
  15. Sara
    I will also say who needs the results. The service manager needs the reviewed weekly comparison, but that does not imply that anyone may receive the underlying data.
  16. Malik
    Define the 8 and the output route. The conditions should remain attached to the analysis even after the calculations are complete and somebody wants to reuse a chart.
  17. Sara
    If we later need customer-level linkage, I will bring a new purpose and field list. This request should not become permission for unrelated analysis.
  18. Malik
    That would be a 9 review. We can assess the new need on its facts, but the current weekly-rate purpose should not become a blanket authorization for every future analysis.
  19. Sara
    My request will list the four fields, rate, workspace, thirty-day access, audience, and required reviews. Approval remains pending.
  20. Malik
    Include the 10 for the aggregate counts as well. That preserves both analytical trust and the handling boundary while giving you the information the actual question needs.

Answers and explanations
  1. processing purpose Turn 2. The processing purpose is the weekly tier-level comparison that determines which fields the analysis needs.
  2. data minimization Turn 4. Data minimization limits the request to sufficient relevant information rather than collecting unrelated personal fields for possible future use.
  3. aggregation Turn 6. Aggregation supplies group-level counts without automatically resolving disclosure risks or approval requirements.
  4. disclosure review Turn 8. Disclosure review assesses sharing risks, including small groups and the proposed audience.
  5. pseudonymization Turn 10. Pseudonymization can replace direct names while preserving possible linkage; it does not automatically establish anonymity or remove controls.
  6. access scope Turn 12. Access scope defines the permitted data and actions, including the approved workspace rather than an assumed unrestricted export.
  7. Access expiry Turn 14. Access expiry ends permission; separate approved rules govern how long the data is retained.
  8. permitted audience Turn 16. The permitted audience identifies who may receive the approved result and prevents internal approval from becoming an unrestricted-sharing assumption.
  9. secondary use Turn 18. Secondary use is a different use beyond the original purpose and may require a new assessment instead of inheriting approval automatically.
  10. data lineage Turn 20. Data lineage records the source and transformations supporting the aggregate counts so their meaning and quality remain traceable.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Sara: I requested the full customer export for weekly late-resolution rates by product tier. I thought extra fields would give us flexibility.
  2. Malik: Start with the processing purpose. The supplied question needs week, tier, eligible-case count, and late-case count. Which part requires names, contact details, or unrestricted free-text comments?
  3. Sara: Those extra fields do not enter the rate. I need late cases divided by eligible cases within each week and tier.

  5. Sara: An aggregate table would work for this comparison. I do not need individual rows if the counts use the agreed definitions and can be traced to their source.
  6. Malik: That aggregation is a useful alternative, subject to review. We still need to check the output, especially where a group is small enough to raise identification or disclosure concerns.

  9. Sara: If someone later asks for individual records, could replacing customer names with stable IDs make that request anonymous and remove the approval requirement?
  10. Malik: That is usually a pseudonymization question, not an automatic anonymity claim. Stable identifiers can still support linkage, and the applicable assessment and access controls must remain explicit.
  11. Sara: For this task, I will stay with the reviewed aggregate fields. The proposal also specifies the approved workspace rather than an unrestricted download to any location.
  12. Malik: Keep that in the access scope. Owner approval is still pending. A technically convenient format does not authorize an export or make the request approved before the decision is recorded.

Dialogue in the book, page 64 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Which customer fields are actually needed?

Fictional governance request using the lesson case: an analyst requests customer-level data without specifying fields or retention duration.

  1. Data steward: Could you specify the fields, analytical purpose, and requested retention period for this customer-level dataset?
  2. Analyst: I asked for the full table, but I haven't explained which variables answer my question.
  3. Data steward: Please narrow that request before access review so privacy and access-control requirements can be assessed.
  4. Analyst: Would removing names mean the entire table is automatically appropriate for my analysis?
  5. Data steward: No. Other fields may remain sensitive or identifying, and authorization still needs to be checked.
  6. Analyst: I'll describe the question and ask whether an aggregated dataset could answer it instead.
  7. Data steward: Good. Also state the requested review timing without treating urgency as approval to bypass governance.
  8. Analyst: I'll submit the scoped request before our planning meeting and identify retention as needing owner confirmation.
  9. Data steward: I'll return any missing-information questions through the approved channel and keep access pending review.
  10. Analyst: Understood. I won't download customer-level records while the authorized scope is still unresolved.
An external collaborator needs a reviewed extract

Fictional analytics collaboration: an external researcher requests transaction-level records; field necessity, authorization, and transfer method are unconfirmed.

  1. Research coordinator: Our collaborator wants the transaction-level file. Could you send it so the analysis can begin?
  2. Data governance lead: First provide the purpose, required fields, recipient details, and proposed retention period for review.
  3. Research coordinator: They already work with us on another project. Does that previous collaboration establish access here?
  4. Data governance lead: Not automatically. This dataset and purpose need their own authorization and appropriate secure-handling assessment.
  5. Research coordinator: Could we ask whether a smaller aggregate extract would meet the same analytical need?
  6. Data governance lead: Yes. That is a useful scope question, but the proposed extract still needs the applicable review.
  7. Research coordinator: I'll request the collaborator's minimum requirements and avoid sending records through ordinary email meanwhile.
  8. Data governance lead: Please include the needed decision timing so the responsible owners can coordinate their response.
  9. Research coordinator: I'll explain that analysis start remains conditional on approved access and transfer arrangements.
  10. Data governance lead: Good. We'll assess a specific request rather than infer permission from an existing professional relationship.
A temporary extract remains in a shared folder

Fictional data review: an extract created for a completed analysis remains in a shared folder; its authorized retention period is unknown.

  1. Analytics manager: The analysis is finished, but its temporary extract is still in the shared folder.
  2. Data steward: Please identify the extract owner and approved retention basis before deciding what should happen next.
  3. Analytics manager: Could we delete it immediately because temporary always means it should disappear after the presentation?
  4. Data steward: We shouldn't invent a retention rule. Check the applicable requirements and any legitimate preservation obligations with the owner.
  5. Analytics manager: The authorized retention period isn't recorded here, so both continued storage and deletion need clarification.
  6. Data steward: Correct. Also confirm current access scope through the established review process rather than assuming the folder is restricted.
  7. Analytics manager: I'll request the retention and access records today and flag the missing documentation explicitly.
  8. Data steward: Thank you. Avoid copying the extract into another location just to make the review easier.
  9. Analytics manager: I'll provide its reference and purpose through the governance channel, keeping the data in place pending direction.
  10. Data steward: Then the authorized owner can decide the appropriate handling based on actual requirements, not a guessed deadline.

Two scenarios to practice

The analysis needs weekly counts, not customer names
  1. Read turns 1-10. Stress the four needed fields and remaining disclosure review.
  2. Switch roles for turns 11-20. Keep access expiry distinct from retention.

A denominator is necessary; a phone number is not

An analyst needs monthly complaint rates by region. The approved design uses month, region, complaint count, and eligible-order count. Names and phone numbers are unnecessary. Access approval is pending; proposed access lasts 14 days.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Analyst: "The rate denominator is the eligible-order ___."

Answer and explanation

B. count

Eligible-order count provides the base for the complaint rate.

2. Steward: "Phone numbers are ___ for this question."

Answer and explanation

C. unnecessary

The briefing explicitly says phone numbers do not support the stated aggregate analysis.

3. Analyst: "The proposed access period is ___ days."

Answer and explanation

A. 14

The supplied local proposal specifies fourteen days of access.

4. Steward: "Approval remains ___."

Answer and explanation

D. pending

The briefing says access approval has not yet been granted.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

REFERENCE
Course: Data Analytics and Business Intelligence English
Professional audience: data analysts, BI developers, analytics engineers, data scientists, reporting teams, business analysts, and data-driven managers
Scope: Original fictional English practice, not statistical certification, legal advice, a privacy determination, or authorization to use customer data. All datasets, thresholds, local approval rules, and business decisions are invented. Actual analysis must follow the applicable definitions, methods, access controls, and responsible review.
Lesson 07: Data Governance and Privacy
Original fictional case: Analyst Sara asks data steward Malik for a full customer export to compare weekly late-resolution rates by product tier. The supplied analysis can use week, tier, eligible-case count, and late-case count; names, emails, phone numbers, and free-text comments are unnecessary. A team proposal uses a reviewed aggregate table in the approved workspace with 30-day access, subject to owner approval and disclosure review. Approval is pending. Small groups still require review. Replacing names with stable IDs would not automatically make row-level data anonymous or remove controls.
Language workshop: The analysis needs weekly counts, not customer names
Communication goal: Specify a purpose-limited data request, distinguish pseudonymization from anonymity, and preserve approval and retention controls.
Language guidance: The rate needs late-case count divided by eligible-case count within each week and tier. Names and contact details do not support that supplied calculation. Data minimization means enough relevant data for the purpose, not an unusably stripped dataset.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): "We are comparing weekly late-resolution rates by tier." / "The calculation needs counts by week and tier." / "Names and contact details are unnecessary for this question." / "Use the reviewed aggregate table." / "Include eligible-case counts as well as late-case counts." / "Owner approval is still pending." / "The work belongs in the approved workspace." / "The proposed access duration is thirty days." / "Access expiry is not automatically the data-retention rule." / "Small groups still need disclosure review." / "Stable IDs do not automatically make the records anonymous." / "Confirm who may receive the output." / "Do not assume approval permits an external export." / "A different question may need a new review." / "Keep the source and transformation lineage." / "Submit the purpose, four fields, workspace, duration, and review conditions."
Vocabulary: data governance: Responsibilities and rules for managing data use. / data steward: A role supporting defined data quality and handling responsibilities. / data owner: The role accountable for specified data decisions. / purpose limitation: Restricting data use to defined appropriate purposes. / data minimization: Limiting data to what the stated purpose needs. / personal data: Information relating to an identifiable person under the applicable rules. / direct identifier: A field that directly identifies a person. / indirect identifier: A field that may identify someone when combined with other information. / pseudonymization: Replacing identifying details while retaining possible linkage. / anonymization: Processing intended to prevent identification under the relevant standard. / re-identification risk: The possibility of connecting data back to an individual. / aggregation: Combining records into group-level summaries. / small cell: A reported group with few contributing observations. / disclosure review: Assessment of risks from releasing or sharing information. / access scope: The specific data and actions a permission covers. / retention period: The duration for which data is kept under defined rules. / access expiry: The point when an access permission ends. / approved workspace: An environment authorized for the stated data work. / data lineage: The traceable path of data through sources and transformations. / data catalog: An organized inventory of datasets and their metadata. / processing purpose: The defined reason for collecting or using data. / permitted audience: The people authorized to receive a result or dataset. / export restriction: A limit on moving data outside an approved environment. / secondary use: A use beyond the original stated purpose.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-10. Stress the four needed fields and remaining disclosure review. Switch roles for turns 11-20. Keep access expiry distinct from retention. Check the transfer. Read the fourteen-day proposal without implying approval already exists.
Partner: Sara: Data analyst / Malik: Data steward
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: An analyst needs monthly complaint rates by region. The approved design uses month, region, complaint count, and eligible-order count. Names and phone numbers are unnecessary. Access approval is pending; proposed access lasts 14 days.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Sara: If we later need customer-level linkage, I will bring a new purpose and field list. This request should not become permission for unrelated analysis.
Malik: That would be a secondary use review. We can assess the new need on its facts, but the current weekly-rate purpose should not become a blanket authorization for every future analysis.
Sara: My request will list the four fields, rate, workspace, thirty-day access, audience, and required reviews. Approval remains pending.
Malik: Include the data lineage for the aggregate counts as well. That preserves both analytical trust and the handling boundary while giving you the information the actual question needs.
END REFERENCE
Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.
