REFERENCE
Course: Data Analytics and Business Intelligence English
Professional audience: data analysts, BI developers, analytics engineers, data scientists, reporting teams, business analysts, and data-driven managers
Scope: Original fictional English practice, not statistical certification, legal advice, a privacy determination, or authorization to use customer data. All datasets, thresholds, local approval rules, and business decisions are invented. Actual analysis must follow the applicable definitions, methods, access controls, and responsible review.
Dialogue 03: Duplicate records
Original fictional setting: An analyst finds a sudden increase after a data join.
Professional roles: Data analyst / Analytics engineer
Published dialogue (reference script, not my own performance):
Data analyst: Revenue doubles after I join orders to shipments. The source order total hasn't changed.
Analytics engineer: Is the join one-to-many? An order can have several shipments.
Data analyst: Yes. I'm repeating the order amount for each shipment row.
Analytics engineer: Aggregate at the intended grain before comparing, and check the join keys.
Data analyst: I'll reconcile distinct orders and revenue to the source totals.
Analytics engineer: Document the grain in the model so future reports don't repeat the same error.
Vocabulary in this exchange: grain: What one row or record represents in a dataset.
END REFERENCE

Course: Data Analytics and Business Intelligence English

Dialogue 04: Clarifying a request
Original fictional setting: A manager asks for every available customer field.
Professional roles: Analytics lead / Marketing manager
Published dialogue (reference script, not my own performance):
Analytics lead: What decision will the customer extract support?
Marketing manager: We want to understand which onboarding step has the highest drop-off.
Analytics lead: Then we may need event counts by step rather than a broad personal-data export.
Marketing manager: Agreed. Could we also compare acquisition channels?
Analytics lead: Yes, subject to data availability and access rules. I'll propose the smallest useful dataset.
Marketing manager: I'll confirm the decision and reporting period so we can review whether the analysis answers the question.
END REFERENCE

For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

Course: Data Analytics and Business Intelligence English
Professional audience: data analysts, BI developers, analytics engineers, data scientists, reporting teams, business analysts, and data-driven managers
Scope: Original fictional English practice, not statistical certification, legal advice, a privacy determination, or authorization to use customer data. All datasets, thresholds, local approval rules, and business decisions are invented. Actual analysis must follow the applicable definitions, methods, access controls, and responsible review.
Dialogue 05: Association and causation
Original fictional setting: A team reviews a regional sales comparison.
Professional roles: Analyst / Regional manager
Published dialogue (reference script, not my own performance):
Analyst: Regions using the new script sold more, but the comparison is observational.
Regional manager: Can we say the script caused the increase?
Analyst: Not from this alone. Those regions also had different staffing and customer mixes.
Regional manager: What would strengthen the evidence?
Analyst: A suitable experimental or carefully justified comparison design, with its limitations explicit.
Regional manager: For now, describe the association accurately and recommend how to evaluate the script more rigorously.
END REFERENCE

TASK: Extend the conversation library
1. Propose six distinct, common scenarios in this field beyond the supplied case or script. For each, name the setting, two or more professional roles, the communication problem and one useful terminology focus. Include routine coordination, clarification, a complication, disagreement, handoff and follow-up where relevant. Choose scenarios that fit this profession, rather than forcing unsuitable situations into it.
2. Ask me to choose one scenario, or request all six in sequence. Stop and wait. Do not write all the scripts before I choose.
3. For the selected scenario, write an original fictional dialogue of 12-18 substantial speaking turns between two or three professionals. Name each role, establish an actual work problem, and let the exchange progress through questions, clarification, competing constraints and a credible next step or explicitly unresolved issue. Use the occupation's natural nomenclature and register. Avoid an interview between a teacher and a learner, generic small talk, and inserting a glossary definition into every reply. Explain specialist terms outside the dialogue instead.
4. After the script, explain five useful expressions in context and identify two grammar or register choices. Give three four-choice questions about the speakers' reasoning, with exactly one answer supported by the dialogue. Wait for my selections, then explain why each answer fits and why its alternatives fail. Add a role-switch reading using the supplied script; do not ask for an essay or invented written reply.
5. Before presenting a script, check names, numbers, chronology, roles and terminology for consistency. Label invented facts and acknowledge uncertain specialist usage. If I requested all six, deliver one complete script at a time and wait for "next". Make each scenario materially different.

Dialogue 06: Experiment imbalance
Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Dialogue 06: Experiment imbalance
Original fictional setting: An analyst checks an experiment before interpreting results.
Professional roles: Experiment analyst / Product lead
Published dialogue (reference script, not my own performance):
Experiment analyst: The allocation ratio differs sharply from the planned split. We need to investigate that first.
Product lead: Could the favorable result still be real?
Experiment analyst: Possibly, but assignment or logging problems could bias it. Significance doesn't fix a broken design.
Product lead: I'll keep the rollout decision pending.
Experiment analyst: I'll review eligibility, assignment, and event completeness with engineering.
Product lead: Bring the validity findings alongside the outcome metrics, so the decision reflects the quality of the evidence.
Vocabulary in this exchange: completeness: The extent to which required information or records are present.
END REFERENCE

Dialogue 07: Data access request
Original fictional setting: A department requests access to a restricted dataset.
Professional roles: Department analyst / Data steward
Published dialogue (reference script, not my own performance):
Department analyst: I need the employee-level dataset for a turnover report. What access is appropriate?
Data steward: Start with the purpose and required fields. An aggregated dataset may meet the need.
Department analyst: I only need trends by department and tenure band.
Data steward: Then let's assess that output and any small-group disclosure concerns under our policy.
Department analyst: I'll submit the request with the audience and retention period.
Data steward: I'll route it for the required approval and confirm the permitted dataset and handling conditions.
Vocabulary in this exchange: retention: Keeping customers, users, staff, or records over a stated period; specify which meaning applies.
END REFERENCE

Dialogue 08: An executive readout
Original fictional setting: An analyst prepares a concise performance explanation.
Professional roles: Senior analyst / Finance director
Published dialogue (reference script, not my own performance):
Senior analyst: Orders fell eight percent, while revenue fell three percent. Higher average order value offset part of the volume decline.
Finance director: Is that price or product mix?
Senior analyst: The current bridge suggests mix, but I still need to reconcile discount effects.
Finance director: Lead with the confirmed movement, then identify that open question.
Senior analyst: I'll include the comparison period, reconciliation status, and the largest contributing categories.
Finance director: Good. Recommend the next analysis without presenting an unverified driver as a settled explanation.
END REFERENCE

All datasets, thresholds, local approval rules, and business decisions are invented. Actual analysis must follow the applicable definitions, methods, access controls, and responsible review.
Lesson 01: Metric Definitions and Business Questions
Original fictional case: Analyst Mei and product lead Rafael compare September figures for the same 2,000 eligible users, using UTC calendar boundaries and excluding internal and test accounts. One report counts 1,200 distinct users with a successful login. Another counts 900 distinct users with a completed transaction. All 900 are within the login group. Both queries match their stated definitions. The business question is how many eligible users completed a transaction, not how many signed in. The team must choose an accurate label and rate.
Language workshop: Two active-user totals, two different events
Communication goal: Reconcile apparently conflicting metrics by stating the event, distinct entity, window, and denominator.
Language guidance: 900/2,000 is 45% of eligible users. 900/1,200 is 75% of users who logged in. Both calculations are valid for their stated bases, but only the first answers the supplied eligible-user question.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): "Are we measuring sign-in or completed transactions?" / "Each user is counted once." / "The qualifying event is a completed transaction." / "Use the September calendar month in UTC." / "Both measures exclude internal and test accounts." / "Both queries match their own definitions." / "The reports use different activity events." / "Nine hundred users answer the transaction question." / "The eligible population is 2,000 users." / "That is 45% of eligible users." / "Seventy-five percent uses logged-in users as its base." / "The transaction group is a subset of the login group." / "Call this transaction-active users." / "Keep the login measure under its own label." / "The metric owner should approve the shared definition." / "We need consistent meaning, not an artificial match between different events."
Vocabulary: metric: A defined quantitative measure. / business question: The decision-relevant question an analysis is meant to answer. / metric definition: Rules specifying exactly how a measure is calculated. / qualifying event: An action meeting the condition for inclusion. / distinct user: A unique user counted once under a defined identity rule. / event count: The number of recorded qualifying occurrences. / eligible population: The complete group allowed into the measure's base. / denominator: The total used as the base of a rate. / numerator: The counted outcome placed over the base of a rate. / reporting window: The time period included in a measure. / calendar month: A month bounded by the adopted calendar and time zone. / time zone: The time reference used to assign event dates. / exclusion rule: A condition removing records from the measure. / internal account: An account belonging to the organization's own personnel or systems. / test account: An account used for testing rather than genuine activity. / deduplication: Removing repeated representations of the same counted entity. / identity resolution: Connecting records that refer to the same entity. / active user: A user meeting a specified activity definition. / login-based activity: Activity defined by a qualifying sign-in. / transaction-based activity: Activity defined by a qualifying transaction. / subset: A group entirely contained within another group. / metric owner: The role accountable for a measure's definition and use. / semantic layer: Shared definitions connecting data to business meaning. / reconciliation: Explaining and resolving differences between reported figures.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-10. Stress qualifying event and denominator as separate choices. Switch roles for turns 11-20. Keep the subset relationship clear when rejecting addition. Check the transfer. Read 40% and 50% with their different population bases.
Partner: Mei: Data analyst / Rafael: Product lead
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A month has 1,000 eligible users, 800 distinct login users, and 400 distinct transacting users, all within the login group. The question asks for transaction activity among all eligible users.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Rafael: Who signs off if the transaction event changes next month? I do not want the same label to hide a new definition.
Mei: Assign a metric owner and record the approved version. That gives both teams a place to resolve changes before comparing totals that no longer mean the same thing.
Rafael: My summary will be nine hundred transaction-active users, forty-five percent of two thousand eligible users, September UTC. The login measure remains separately labeled.
Mei: That completes the reconciliation. We have preserved both correct calculations, selected the measure that answers the decision, and removed the ambiguity that created the apparent conflict.
END REFERENCE

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Paste into a new chat, then follow the exercise one step at a time. English Ladder does not send anything to an AI service. Your chosen service's terms and any usage charges apply.

AI responses can contain mistakes. Compare feedback with the lesson and check specialist claims against the course references. Use these exercises for language practice; follow qualified guidance for actual professional decisions.

For teachers and study partners

Ask learners to respond before revealing the model. Give feedback on one meaning issue and one language pattern, then let them repeat. For mixed levels, offer the frames first and remove them in the second round.

Use the learner book for extended dialogue practice, vocabulary and collocations, reusable phrases, and explained answer keys.
